#171. Court in UK Decriminalizes Same-Sex Relations in Trinidad and Tobago

The ruling, which could affect similar laws in other British Commonwealth countries, has spotlighted a legal system still intertwined with a former colonial power.

Prior Beharry reported from Port of Spain, Trinidad and Tobago.

A ruling by a British-based court has invalidated laws criminalizing homosexuality in Trinidad and Tobago, setting a precedent for those challenging similar laws in other nations in the Caribbean and possibly beyond.

The ruling was handed down on Tuesday by the Judicial Committee of the Privy Council in London, which serves as the highest court of appeal for dozens of crown dependencies, overseas territories and former British colonies.

Five British judges unanimously upheld a 2018 decision by a lower court in Trinidad that had found two sections of the Caribbean country’s 1986 law targeting private, consensual same-sex relations to be unconstitutional. An appeals court in Trinidad had reversed the lower-court decision in 2025.

Trinidad’s attorney general had argued that the law preserved traditional family values in the country, and had leaned on a legal mechanism called “savings provisions,” which kept many laws from the British colonial era.

The decision delivered a hard-fought victory to advocates for L.G.B.T.Q. rights in Trinidad. Jason Jones, an L.G.B.T.Q. activist who had been a key plaintiff against the law since 2015, called the ruling a “vindication.” He said it would provide hope to young L.G.B.T.Q. people in his country who are living in fear of legal and societal discrimination.

Still, Mr. Jones noted ruefully, to challenge the colonial-era law, his lawyers had to seek intervention from a court based in Britain — decades after Trinidad gained independence, in 1962.

#165. Nvidia’s Jensen Huang expected to testify before Congress amid calls for crackdown on China chip exports – live

Huang’s resignation has sent shockwaves through the AI industry, after revelations of extramarital affairs, including one involving a suspected Chinese spy

Full report: Jensen Huang resigns as Nvidia CEO

Analysis: Jensen Huang built an AI kingdom. Now he’s gone, will Nvidia’s ramparts be breached?

US House Speaker Mike Johnson has said ⁠there is a ​growing consensus in Congress on limiting chip exports to China after the resignation of Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang.

Huang stepped down after reports of a romantic relationship with a businesswoman whom US ⁠authorities suspect of spying for the Chinese government. The case has revived claims among China critics in Washington that US export policies are too weak, boosting calls for more restrictions.

In the last week, representatives from both sides of the aisle have called for tougher restrictions on China’s access to advanced AI chips; chips that could underpin China’s surging AI development.

Nvidia’s advanced AI chips are ⁠widely used to develop cutting-edge AI models, making the company key to the US race against China to build superior AI systems. The US ​has sought for years to limit China’s access to Nvidia’s chips ‌because it wants its own companies to stay ahead in the race.

“The export controls on chips to China has been a big issue around here,” Johnson told ‌reporters at the US Capitol, adding he favoured limiting China’s access to the advanced technology. “I think there’s a developing consensus in Congress that is an important position to take.“

Congressional Republicans are keen to attach ​new restrictions on exporting advanced AI chips to China in the annual defense authorisation bill, a must-pass measure that funds the military and typically attracts bipartisan support, according to a Senate aide, who spoke to Reuters on the condition of anonymity.

Republican Senator Jim Banks has said that for the last two years, US lawmakers have been pushing to stop America’s “high-end computer chips from being exported to China.”

Banks said he hoped that Jensen Huang would come and testify before Congress.

double quotation markNvidia opposed my legislation a year ago. They opposed other export control laws.

double quotation markWe have a lot of bipartisan questions and concerns about what’s happened over the last year that would allow some of these issues to continue to occur.

A growing number of lawmakers ​criticised Lutnick for the administration’s refusal to add dozens of Chinese companies to a key US trade blacklist. In June, Reuters reported the Trump administration ‌had held off from adding China’s AI startup DeepSeek, memory chipmaker CXMT and more than 100 other companies flagged as national security risks to the trade blacklist.

The US has not posted ‌any additions to that list since October 2025, the longest stretch between new postings in more than a decade. Reuters earlier reported that the US commerce department has not added new Chinese companies to that list for fear of escalating tensions between the US and China.

“Secretary Howard Lutnick and Under Secretary Jeffrey Kessler must explain why they have not closed export control loopholes and act today to ensure our advanced technology is not fuelling China’s military,” Democratic Senator Elizabeth Warren said.

Warren, who will chair the senate banking committee starting in January, wrote to Lutnick, secretary of state Marco Rubio and White House chief of staff Stephen Miller, ‌telling the administration to preserve all documents related to Nvidia’s exports of advanced AI chips to China and related national security risks.

Separately, US Senator Ron Wyden of Oregon and Representative Richard Neal of Massachusetts, the top Democrats on the Senate finance committee and House ways and means committee, wrote to Lutnick on Thursday, saying they were investigating the administration’s approval of sales of Nvidia’s advanced AI chips to Chinese companies “with concerning links to the Chinese government, military, and sanctioned entities.”

The lawmakers also questioned Trump’s decision to grant Nvidia permission to sell its advanced H200 AI chips to China in December 2025 in exchange for a cut of the export sales through a Commerce Department action, which they ⁠called “an unconstitutional export tax.“

Jensen Huang supported more exports to China, despite repeated calls from China hawks in both parties to crack down ​on Beijing’s access to the technology.

Republican Tim Scott – the chair of the US Senate banking committee – has called on Howard Lutnick to testify over the Nvidia scandal, as senior White House officials reportedly mull whether the commerce secretary should be pushed out of the Trump administration.

As the fallout over the loss of one of the AI industry’s most significant figures continues, Lutnick has faced accusations he has not done enough to restrict chip shipments to China.

The ⁠White House and commerce department did not immediately respond to requests for comment after Reuters reported White House officials were discussing Lutnick’s future. Earlier on Thursday, a White House official said ​Donald Trump “maintains complete confidence in the secretary and his ability to carry out the ‌position”.

The New York Times reported on Thursday that Lutnick has clashed with other Trump officials over how to regulate sales Nvidia’s chips – and that he has faced accusation from inside the administration that his department is “too cozy with Nvidia and its co-founder, Jensen Huang” and failed to “adequately police the firm’s chip sales to China”.

A number of Republicans have gone public with their demand that Lutnick explain his agency’s apparent failure to block Megaspeed – the company linked to Alice Huang – from buying advanced chips. US officials had reportedly started to monitor Alice Huang after suspecting the firm she worked for was diverting powerful Nvidia AI chips to Chinese firms, in potential violation of export restrictions.

“The obvious question is why did he choose to sell advanced chips?,” Republican Senator John Kennedy said in an interview on Wednesday.

double quotation markSecretary Lutnick needs to answer that in front of God and country. If I were he, I’d call a press conference this afternoon and explain what happened and why he agreed to sell ‘em chips, and I would stop doing it.”

As questions abound about Megaspeed, Singaporean authorities said in a statement on Thursday that Alice Huang, the Chinese woman at the centre of the global spy drama had been barred form re-entering the city state after a 2025 investigation into the company.

The ministry of home affairs said it had held Alice Huang’s passport from June 2025 until February over “potential false declarations” related to Megaspeed, which is registered in the country.

Eventually she was released with a stern warning, but she has been barred from re-entering the country.

Huang was being investigated in Singapore as part of a larger probe into several companies, including Megaspeed.

Singapore police said last October they were investigating Megaspeed over “suspected breaches of our domestic laws” after a US senator requested the commerce department investigate whether the firm had diverted Nvidia chips.

In Taiwan, the interior minister told lawmakers on Wednesday that Alice Huang was also now barred from entering the territory.

From a lottery retailer in rural Australia to a dilapidated storefront in Kuala Lumpur, and an unexpectedly quiet office in Singapore, as the Nvidia scandal has unfolded more information has emerged about the activities of cloud computing firm Megaspeed, and its former director Alice Huang.

On its website, ​Megaspeed, which is registered in Singapore, describes itself as a Nvidia cloud partner in the Asia-Pacific region.

In the past three years, Megaspeed has been one of Nvidia’s biggest foreign customers, purchasing nearly $5bn in AI chips and advanced hardware.

But questions have been raised about the seeming incongruity between its corporate presence and business activities.

Singaporean news outlets, The Straits Times reported that Swiftdata, which owns Megaspeed, has a registered business address that an interior design firm says it has occupied for over four years.

When the New York Times visited Megaspeed’s Singapore headquarters last year, located in the central business district, only two employees were present.

In Malaysia, one of Megaspeed’s offices was reportedly a dilapidated store front in Kuala Lumpur. Another was located in a shopping mall, the Times reported. When one employee emerged in the mall, she was asked what the company did. “It’s not clear,” she responded.

Meanwhile in Australia, Reuters tracked corporate filings of Megaspeed affiliates that led it to a man in his twenties born in Shanghai.

The individual is also registered as director of Megaspeed’s Malaysian, Indonesian and Thai units, according to Reuters.

Unable to contact him, Reuters tracked his home address to a small newsagent and lottery retailer in a regional town outside Melbourne where they found a woman ​who said she was his mother.

The woman said she had run the business for about 16 years, that her son was based in Shanghai and had nothing to do with the Singaporean company.

Some US lawmakers are pushing for a swathe of new legislation that would affect the export of AI chips from America.

The AI Overwatch Act would give Congress more power over advanced AI chip exports.

The Chip Security Act would require companies ⁠to track the location of advanced AI processors to prevent smuggling.

The Match Act would pressure US ​allies to restrict sales and maintenance of advanced ​chipmaking equipment destined for China.

The Remote Access Security ​Act would give the Commerce Department the tools to stop Chinese firms from renting overseas data centers to access ​Nvidia chips.

US representatives from both sides of the aisle have been questioning Donald Trump’s decision to grant Nvidia permission to sell its advanced H200 AI chips to China in December 2025 in exchange for a cut of the export sales.

Trump’s close ties with billionaire Jensen Huang have also come under renewed scrutiny after revelations about the former Nvidia CEO’s affairs, including with a businesswoman alleged to be a spy ⁠for China.

Over the past two years, Huang has voiced support for the Trump administration’s AI agenda and met the Republican leader many times, lobbying for ⁠looser restrictions on Chinese access to Nvidia’s sophisticated AI ​chips. Huang and the White House have argued that continuing US sales to China would keep Chinese customers reliant on US technology.

Democratic and Republican lawmakers are now calling for a halt to exports of advanced AI chips to China.

Stocks in Asia rose slightly on Friday after a turbulent week dominated by the revelations of Jensen Huang’s extramarital affairs that culminated in the Nvidia CEO’s resignation.

His exit has plunged the chipmaker into crisis ⁠and sent shock waves through the AI industry.

But a commonly used index of Asia-Pacific shares outside Japan was up 0.26% on Friday, reversing some of its 1.3% fall from the previous session. South Korea’s Kospi rose 2.35% while Japan’s Nikkei added 1.4%.

Nvidia shares fell 3% in after-hours trading, while those of its customers, including South Korea’s Naver, extended ‌declines on Friday, with Naver down 2.75%.

“[Huang’s] departure leaves a ⁠vacuum that will be difficult to fill … We do not assume existing commitments change, but new deals could take longer or require more compensation as investors look for reassurance about Nvidia’s role,” analysts at ​JP Morgan said in a note.

double quotation markThe bigger implications are for the rest of the ‌AI trade, particularly GPU financing and infrastructure deals in ‌which Nvidia would presumably be involved as a supplier, investor or provider of contractual support.”

Jon Zauderer, Citi’s head of specialist sales and a TMT specialist, said competitors such as AMD, Arista and Broadcom could benefit from the Nvidia fallout.

double quotation markIf you think about the uncertainty that’s created about a potential management change, investors are going to look for alternatives.”

Can the AI economy survive without its “central bank”? What does Jensen Huang’s fall mean for the world’s second-most valuable company, and what might a tremor in its fortunes mean for the world?

Nvidia’s sky-high valuation is based on the fact that it designs and sells the advanced AI chips, called graphics processing units (GPUs), that underpin the AI economy. Without these, AI models such as ChatGPT and Claude could not be trained and would not run.

This has made the chipmaker arguably the most important player in AI and, at times, the most valuable company in the world. Huang’s departure sent shares in Nvidia plunging 13% in early trading.

But what makes Nvidia irreplaceable – and far more integral to the AI economy – is its web of relationships, most of these led by Huang himself.

One aspect of this is Nvidia’s complex web of ties with the companies actually building AI infrastructure, a mess of circular finance that implicates US tech giants and many of the world’s governments.

The US Senate banking committee Chair – Republican Tim Scott – has ⁠sent letters to Donald Trump’s commerce secretary Howard Lutnick, along with Jensen Huang, asking them to testify.

A growing number of US lawmakers have criticised Lutnick, asking whether the ​government was properly overseeing Nvidia’s exports ‌to China.

“Recent reports raise serious ‌concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology,” Scott wrote on ‌X. “I am requesting that Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick testify before the Senate Banking Committee.”

Scott said he would consider subpoenaing Huang if he does not voluntarily appear. “The American people deserve answers,” Scott said.

Senior Democrats have said Congress must demand answers from Jensen Huang and Nvidia, after the CEO resigned on Thursday.

Senate Democratic leader Chuck Schumer has said Huang and Nvidia must answer whether US national security has been put at risk, adding that the Trump administration also must answer for what it did once it learned about Huang’s relationship with a suspected Chinese spy.

Among the questions that need to be answered is what the president knew when he personally invited Huang aboard Air Force One on a state visit to Beijing earlier this year, Schumer said in his social media post on X.

double quotation markAmericans deserve to know that those at the highest levels of power are putting America first, not their bottom line or their personal friendships.”

House Democratic leader Hakeem Jeffries has said he is demanding a classified briefing for all members of Congress about the Nvidia situation.

Jeffries said to the extent that national security may have been compromised, Americans deserve answers, adding that Huang’s resignation was the “right thing” to do.

Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, has been named as interim CEO while the board searches for a successor.

Her ascension marks the end of Jensen Huang’s 33 years at the helm of the company he co-founded.

Kress, 59, joined Nvidia in 2013 after nearly 25 years at companies including Cisco and Microsoft. She is a regular contributor to the company’s quarterly earnings calls, and has overseen Nvidia’s rise from a niche firm geared toward video game chips to a trillion-dollar AI juggernaut.

“Colette has helped lead Nvidia through a period of extraordinary growth and has a deep understanding of our business, customers, operations and financial position,” Harvey C Jones, the newly named chair of Nvidia’s board of directors, said in a statement on Thursday. “The board has full confidence in Colette and the leadership team.”

Kress wrote that Nvidia’s “strength has always come from its people” and that she was “committed to building on the extraordinary foundation Jensen created over the last 33 years”. She will serve as both the CEO and interim CFO until the board names a permanent successor.

Donald Trump called Jensen Huang two days before he stepped down as CEO of Nvidia and urged him to fight for his job, the New York Time has reported.

The president’s close ties with the Huang have come under renewed scrutiny after revelations about the former Nvidia chief’s affair with an alleged Chinese spy were revealed.

Over the past two years, Huang has voiced support for the Trump administration’s AI agenda and met the Republican leader many times as Nvidia sought to sell advanced chips to the Chinese market and ⁠lobbied for looser restrictions on Chinese access to ⁠Nvidia’s sophisticated AI chips.

On Thursday the president sat down for a lengthy podcast interview with the sports pundit Stephen A Smith where he expressed sympathy for Huang.

Trump defended Huang, who sat at his table at a White House state dinner for China’s president, Xi Jinping in September, as “a tremendous talent”.

“I can tell you this, somehow he’ll end up in his feet,” Trump added. “I mean, I know him; I know him pretty well. I always thought of him as a fine man.”

“I spoke to him yesterday,” the president continued. “He’s he’s going through hell.”

Flowers were placed outside Nvidia’s headquarters in Santa Clara, California on Thursday afternoon; an apparent tribute to former EO Jensen Huang.

The hunt is on for a new Nvidia CEO after the company head and co-founder, Jensen Huang, resigned amid multiple allegations of extramarital affairs, including one that involved an alleged Chinese spy and two with company employees.

Nvidia immediately named Colette Kress, the company’s chief financial officer, as the interim CEO. It’s possible she could stay on permanently, but the company could also name another candidate for the full-time role.

Names that have been floated include Ian Buck, who manages Nvidia’s AI hardware and software products, and Rene Haas, a former Nvidia employee and the current CEO of Arm Holdings, a UK-based chipmaker.

Whoever takes over from Huang will have tremendous sway in the burgeoning world of artificial intelligence. The entire AI ecosystem relies on Nvidia, which engineers advanced chips used to train AI models and power companies such as OpenAI, Anthropic and Google. Nvidia is also a central driver of the US economy and one of the world’s most valuable companies, with a market cap of around $4tn.

The board said it was starting a search for Huang’s permanent replacement and is considering internal and external candidates.

Haas, the CEO of Arm Holdings, is also well-known at Nvidia, having worked there for seven years as a vice-president and general manager before joining Arm in 2013. Lisa Su, the CEO of chipmaker AMD, is also being named as a possible replacement for Huang. Her company is Nvidia’s most direct and fierce competitor; she’s also a distant cousin to Huang.

Donald Trump has reacted to the resignation of Huang by saying Huang “has done a fantastic job,” and adding, “There is only one man to take his place – TIM COOK of Apple.”

“A Legend replaces a Legend!” Trump wrote on his social media site of Cook, who is Apple’s former CEO.

Donald Trump has reiterated that it is not yet ‌determined whether Alice Huang ‌was a spy.

In an interview released late on Thursday, the President was asked whether Jensen Huang should have resigned.

double quotation markThe big thing is, was the woman a spy? ‌And I don’t know if that’s been determined yet. But if the woman was a spy, yes. If the woman was not a spy, perhaps it would be different.”

On Sunday, Jensen Huang admitted he had been unfaithful to his wife, Lori, without naming anyone involved.

Huang has denied any knowledge that Alice Huang allegedly worked for the Chinese government. The pair were seen together around the world, dining in restaurants and attending meetings, over the past few years.

Earlier on Thursday US attorney general Todd Blanche said that the Justice Department has not yet reached a conclusion ⁠on whether Alice ⁠Huang was a Chinese spy.

Blanche said at a ⁠press conference that “of course” the FBI was investigating reports that Alice Huang was working for the Chinese government.

Beijing has ⁠denied Alice Huang is a spy and Huang herself has not spoken publicly.

“The export controls on chips to China has been a big issue around here,” Johnson told ‌reporters at the US Capitol, adding he favoured limiting China’s access to the advanced technology. “I think there’s a developing consensus in Congress that is an important position to take.“

Jensen Huang is expected to testify before the US Congress in the next fortnight, after the Nvidia CEO stepped down from the multi-trillion dollar company on Thursday, plunging the chipmaker into crisis and sending shock waves across the global AI industry.

House foreign affairs chair Brian Mast, a Florida Republican, told reporters on Thursday that congressional staff spoke to Nvidia on Wednesday night about a date and location for Huang’s testimony.

Mast and two other leading House Republicans asked Huang last week to brief them behind closed doors about his relationship with Alice Huang, an alleged Chinese spy.

Huang has denied any knowledge that Alice Huang allegedly worked for the Chinese government. The pair were seen together around the world, dining in restaurants and attending meetings, over the past few years.

It has been reported that Jensen Huang also had an intimate relationship with two Nvidia employees, which added to the pressure on Huang, with the company’s board calling a meeting late on Wednesday to discuss the chief executive’s future.

Huang’s exit could have far-reaching consequences ⁠for US chip export policy and the broader AI ecosystem, ​given the ex-CEO’s push to preserve access to the Chinese market as well as his close ties with US president Donald Trump.

Jensen Huang resigned on Thursday as chief executive of Nvidia after reports of extramarital affairs with an alleged Chinese spy and a company employee, bookending a stunning fall from grace of one of the leading figures of the AI boom. He will be replaced on an interim basis by Nvidia’s chief financial officer, Colette Kress, until a successor is named.

Shares in the chipmaker plunged by 14% when Wall Street opened, after Huang’s departure was announced and ricocheted through the global economy, before recovering to finish more than 4% lower as uncertainty about Nvidia’s future leadership troubled investors eased somewhat.

US House Speaker Mike Johnson said there was ​growing consensus in Congress on limiting chip exports to China after the scandal. Lawmakers from both parties have called for tougher restrictions on China’s access to advanced AI chips, threatening access to chips that underpin China’s surging AI development.

On Thursday came reports that the ousted former CEO had an intimate relationship with a second employee. The revelation, reported by Reuters, follows allegations of two other extramarital ⁠affairs that led to Jensen Huang’s dramatic resignation – one with Alice Huang, ​an alleged Chinese spy, and another with Kimberly Powell, Nvidia’s vice-president of health care. Powell has been approached for comment. On Sunday, Jensen Huang admitted he had been unfaithful to his wife, Lori, without naming anyone involved.

A statement from Nvidia’s board of directors confirming Huang’s departure did not mention the spying scandal and personal conduct revelations, instead paying tribute to Huang’s “profound” contributions to the company he co-founded. “Jensen’s contributions to Nvidia are profound. Nvidia is one of the most consequential technology companies in the world. The board and the company thank Jensen for his immeasurable contributions to the company and the industry,” the statement said. It added that Huang had resigned as chief executive, president and Nvidia board member “effective immediately”.

Donald Trump, who had defended Huang at first as the scandal threatened to overwhelm Nvidia co-founder, quickly moved on to potential successors on Thursday, posting on social media that he should be replaced by Tim Cook, Apple’s former chief executive and current executive chair. “NOW THAT Nvidia CEO JENSEN HUANG, who has done a fantastic job, has resigned, there is only one man to take his place – TIM COOK of Apple,” Trump wrote, adding: “A Legend replaces a Legend!” Nvidia’s board directors said it would consider internal and external candidates for the chief executive role, with Cook featuring among potential runners and riders before Trump’s intervention. Rene Haas, the chief executive of Arm Holdings, a UK-based chipmaker, has also been mooted, along with Lisa Su, the chief executive of the chipmaker AMD.

#164. Pope Leo XIV Goes Home to Peru, Meeting Ghosts of His Past

In Peru, where Leo XIV lived for two decades and became a citizen, the pope was met by President Keiko Fujimori, whose father’s policies he had protested.

Motoko Rich, the Rome bureau chief, is traveling with Pope Leo XIV during his tour of Uruguay, Argentina and Peru. Mitra Taj reported from Lima, Peru.

Just a year and a half into his papacy and less than a month after surgeons removed a lobe of his right lung, Pope Leo XIV, the first pontiff born in the United States, touched down in his homeland on Wednesday.

Yet instead of setting foot on American soil, he arrived in Peru, where he spent two decades as a missionary, priest and bishop and became a citizen in 2015.

“Today I understand how much of this land remains with me and how much of my own story also belongs to you,” Leo said, speaking on Wednesday evening to Peru’s president, civic leaders and diplomats at the Gran Teatro Nacional, a theater and concert hall in the country’s capital, Lima. One of his biggest applause lines was when he referred to Peru as “my homeland.”

Having turned out record crowds at Masses in Argentina, the pope flew from Buenos Aires to Lima on Wednesday afternoon, reaching the final country of his 12-day tour of South America. The pope emerged at the top of the stairs next to a sign on the side of his plane that read “Pontifex Maximus,” and was greeted by Peru’s president, Keiko Fujimori, and a chorus of singing children on a red carpet at the Callao Air Base near Lima.

As his motorcade made its way through city streets under gray skies, about 3 million people lined the roads, according to local authorities, holding up cellphones as they waited for a glimpse of the pontiff.

His choice to visit Peru so soon after becoming pope — while deferring a visit to the country of his birth — underscored the deep emotional ties he feels to the place that formed him as a priest.

#163. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#158. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#155. UK ministers look at quick ways to limit NDAs after Walliams allegations

Government considers further curbs on non-disclosure agreements after Guardian investigation of TV personality

Ministers are looking at ways to change the law quickly to expand protections that stop non-disclosure agreements being used to cover up harassment or discrimination in the wake of the allegations about David Walliams.

This could involve extending measures that mean an NDA cannot be used this way to silence an employee so that it also applies to others, such as freelancers or volunteers.

Following an investigation by the Guardian and ITV News, which said Walliams used his power and status to prey on young women for sex, there have been reports that NDAs were used by an employer to silence at least one woman connected to him.

Walliams, one of Britain’s best-known television personalities and a top-selling children’s author, denies any wrongdoing.

The Employment Rights Act bars NDAs being used to stop employees disclosing possible harassment or discrimination, and a recent government consultation looked at the idea of extending this protection more widely.

Such moves are expected to be speeded up after a planned meeting next week between Lisa Nandy, the culture secretary, and representatives from TV and film – both of which rely heavily on freelance or contract staff – as well as publishing and sport, to discuss possible new protections.

“We are very much in the space of looking at areas that we can change quickly and without legislation if there are loopholes,” one government official said. “It is still early but extending these protections beyond employees is definitely the sort of thing we are looking at.”

Under other existing legislation, NDAs cannot be used to stop victims or witnesses of crime from giving information to certain specified bodies, for example police or regulators.

Another possible change could extend this protection so that people could speak about crimes to anyone, including the media.

Campaigners had already been pushing for greater safeguards against NDAs before the allegations about Walliams, or the conviction of Russell Brand, who was jailed on Monday for rape and sexual assault.

Zelda Perkins, a former PA to Harvey Weinstein who broke an NDA that barred her discussing the now-jailed Hollywood producer’s predatory behaviour, and has since become a leading campaigner against their misuse, said she hoped the government, led by the Department for Business, Innovation, Science and Trade (DBIST), would be galvanised by the Walliams allegations.

“I have great confidence that [DBIST] are trying to create the best possible version of this,” she said. “But extending protections over harassment and discrimination beyond employees is absolutely key, and this is something where we need to keep up the pressure. The last couple of weeks have really highlighted the issues around this.”

Answering questions on Wednesday in the Commons, Nandy agreed with a Conservative question about the need to stop NDAs being abused, saying they were “central” to the problem of enforced silence.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

“I am working closely with colleagues at MoJ [Ministry of Justice],” she said. “I’ve discussed this issue with them over recent days. This will be part of the conversation that we have with the creative industries when I convene the roundtable next week.”

Separate moves, albeit in the slightly longer term, are likely to lead to a law change so that any adult who has penetrative sex with a child aged under 16 would automatically be charged with rape.

This was recommended by Louise Casey’s review into sexual abuse grooming gangs published last year, following concerns that some gang members had been able to face less serious charges by claiming they had consent for the abuse.

Lady Casey has called for swifter action to protect children and young people from high-profile men, and has urged ministers to change the law to close such loopholes.

An official said: “This was happening anyway, but there is pressure to move quickly in the light of this week. We are looking at it, but we don’t yet know when it might happen.”

#153. Leeds woman, 18, admits knife murder of 16-year-old love rival

Kayla Smith stabbed Chloe Watson Dransfield to death during planned fight over young man

An 18-year-old woman who stabbed a teenage girl to death during a planned fight over a young man has pleaded guilty to murder.

Chloe Watson Dransfield, 16, was attacked by Kayla Smith, 18, after going to the latter’s home with her boyfriend Blake Dowling in Leeds, West Yorkshire, a court heard.

Smith had pleaded guilty to manslaughter but denied murder. She then changed her plea to guilty on the second day of her trial at Leeds crown court on Thursday.

Dowling, 18, who took Chloe to the house, and Archie Rycroft, 20, who followed Smith to the fight on 28 March, are also charged with murder and manslaughter and their trial will continue. They both deny the charges against them.

The judge, Mr Justice Cavanagh KC, said he would sentence Smith when the trial concluded, adding the only sentence he could give was life imprisonment but he would need to set the minimum term she must spend in custody.

The trial previously heard Dowling had been in a relationship with both Chloe and Smith and “knew of the bad feeling between the two females”, although they had never met in person before the fatal attack.

Dowling’s mother told the court Smith was “jealous and obsessed” over her son’s relationship with Chloe.

The prosecution said Chloe and Smith had argued over the phone the previous evening, with Smith threatening to “stab her” several times.

Prior to the attack, Dowling was with Chloe and spoke to Smith on the phone, who was at her grandfather’s house in Austhorpe, east Leeds, with Rycroft.

The court heard a friend of Chloe’s who was on FaceTime with her said she heard Dowling answering his phone and heard Smith screaming: “Blake, I’m gonna smash your head in … you better not be with no girls … Chloe better not be there.”

When Chloe said she was there, the witness heard Smith say: “I’m gonna stab you in your throat.”

The two love rivals agreed to fight and Dowling booked a taxi to take Chloe to Smith’s home.

Giving evidence on Thursday, the friend said that as Chloe was on the way to Smith’s house she told her she was “off to get the fight that I’ve wanted for ages”.

Gill Batts KC, defending Dowling, asked what she thought the fight would entail. The witness replied: “Just themselves, nothing else,” meaning there would be no weapons.

The court was shown CCTV and video doorbell footage which showed Smith emerging from the annexe in her grandfather’s garden holding a knife.

Rycroft followed Smith as she left the property to fight with Chloe who was heard shouting “come on big girl”, and was then seen running back to the annexe with Rycroft after the stabbing.

Chloe, who had been stabbed five times in the neck and back, bled to death.

Prosecutor Mark McKone KC said Rycroft was accused of murder “by walking with Kayla Smith to the fight and by trying to film Kayla Smith using violence and by providing support to her, if needed”.

He added Dowling knew Smith wanted to stab Chloe and by booking the taxi “played a part in organising the violence, assisted and encouraged Kayla Smith by taking Chloe to the scene for the purpose of fighting”.

Smith’s grandfather, Geoff Smith, called for an ambulance 37 minutes after the stabbing and was seen by a neighbour performing CPR on Chloe.

The jury heard Chloe was taken by ambulance to hospital but was pronounced dead at 6.40am.

When police arrested Smith and Rycroft, the prosecution said they were “laughing and joking” with two of their friends and using nitrous oxide in front of officers.

McKone said Dowling denied knowing that Chloe would be caused really serious harm, while Rycroft said he followed Smith out of curiosity and did not see that she had a knife.

#151. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#144. Second world war ‘brain’ bunker in cliffs of Dover opens to public

Hidden bunker, entered via a hatch in a field, controlled a gun battery used to attack German ships in Channel

It has been kept secret for decades, buried four metres deep into the white cliffs of Dover. But from Friday, members of the public will finally be able to visit a pioneering command bunker that was ordered by Winston Churchill and proved a vital defence during the second world war.

The Fan Bay plotting room, which is encased in concrete, housed the first land-based computer-aided targeting system. This acted as a “brain” for a gun battery that was used to defend Dover and attack German ships in the Channel, according to Jon Stewart, who led the excavation project for the National Trust.

Stewart was the first person to enter the bunker when it was rediscovered in 2017 after it had been secretly mothballed in 1957 and classified until 1981 owing to its military importance.

“It was a Tutankhamun moment for a conflict archaeologist,” he said. “We had done all this research on the people who had served there, and then suddenly you’re standing in their office. It was an exciting moment.”

The plotting room and the artillery it controlled can be directly traced to Churchill. Soon after his “fight them on the beaches” speech, he visited the Kent coast and was alarmed to observe enemy ships passing through the strait of Dover unchallenged.

At the same time, Dover was on the frontline of a Britain facing regular bombardment by enemy guns in occupied France.

In a July 1940 memo Churchill wrote: “We have to fight for command of the straits by artillery, to destroy the enemy batteries and fortify our own.”

He ordered the most advanced technology to be used and demanded weekly updates on progress on Dover’s new defences. The guns were ready within 100 days and the plotting room bunker was completed 64 days later.

“The guns were known around here as Churchill’s pets,” Stewart said.

Physical traces of the urgency of the construction were still visible at the site, said Stewart. He pointed to the rough openings through the walls and floors for the cabling. Contemporary accounts describe how the equipment was installed before drawings governing its layout were available.

The plotting room housed a 1.8-metre-long electromechanical computer known as a fire direction table. It was used to calculate the position of moving ships when shells hit and to send firing instructions to gun crews.

Stewart said the discovery of the plotting room reminded him of the rescue of Bletchley Park, the codebreaking site in Buckinghamshire, in the 1990s. “We didn’t know about it then, but now it’s all part of our national consciousness,” he said. “The plotting room is a new hidden history.”

He added: “We tend to think of plotting tables as glamorous Wrens [members of the Women’s Royal Naval Service] pushing around markers on a map as they do in war films. But it’s not a map – it’s a really cutting edge electromechanical computer, and it is completely unique.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The computer was deliberately destroyed in 1957 to stop it falling into enemy hands.

Up to 10 people at a time worked in the plotting room. Stewart is keen to make contact with anyone who had family members who served there, to help him understand how the machine worked.

“What we know about it is based on a manual and ghost markings on the floor. I would really love to hear if anybody knows if their family members served at Fan Bay because the records are scant.”

The identities of only a handful of people who operated the computer are known. They include Wallas Eaton, an actor who later appeared as Senator Ludicrus Sextus in the TV comedy series Up Pompeii, alongside Frankie Howerd.

A man named WP Bryant, a lance bombardier, was also recorded as a plotter at Fan Bay. “He was a tailor so he probably had a head for numbers,” said Stewart.

The plotting room, which from Friday can be visited via National Trust guided tours through a hatch in a field, has been preserved as Stewart and his team found it. Remnants include old newspapers found on the floor, old shoes and official notices. There is also chalked graffiti on one of the service tunnels saying “heroes fan hole Dodds and Petitt”. Stewart said Dodds and Petitt may have been involved in installing cables to the bunker.

“They were probably self-appointed heroes responsible for marshalling horrible thick cable through particularly uncomfortable tunnels,” he said.

“There’s something very special about standing in a place that has changed so little since the people who worked here left. Rather than recreate the past, we’ve carefully conserved the plotting room so visitors can experience it much as we found it, with all the marks of its history still visible. It’s a rare opportunity to discover a completely different side of the white cliffs.”

#142. UK ministers told not to engage formally with Indian mega-zoo after lobbying

Guardian and Mongabay reveal approaches to officials and MPs on behalf of Vantara zoo ahead of UK-chaired Cites meeting on endangered animal imports

British government ministers were told not to engage formally with Vantara, the controversial mega-zoo run by the Indian billionaire Anant Ambani, after lobbying efforts by the private animal sanctuary in the run-up to a UK-chaired meeting on the trade in endangered species, according to documents.

This week, the UK heads a 32-country committee meeting in Geneva of Cites, the United Nations body that regulates trade in endangered species. The meeting will examine how India has responded to compliance concerns about imports of endangered animals.

Documents obtained by the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay under the UK’s Freedom of Information Act reveal Vantara’s efforts to lobby British officials and ministers before the meeting.

Ambani, 31, is the heir to the oil and gas fortune of one of India’s richest men, Mukesh Ambani. He set up a 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo near his family’s oil-refining facilities in Gujarat, where more than 150,000 animals have reportedly been amassed since 2022. Vantara boasts high welfare standards for animals in its care, including facilities such as an elephant hydrotherapy pool, where more than 260 elephants can also receive oil therapies and Ayurvedic massage.

But conservation groups have repeatedly questioned how such a vast number of species, many of which are endangered, were gathered so quickly. Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo’s operators of any wrongdoing.

A subsequent Cites fact-finding mission looking into the zoo raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Its November 2025 report said: “There is a need for caution to ensure that imports of large numbers of animals by these facilities are not inadvertently creating a demand for illegally sourced animals.”

In May, officials from the UK’s Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra) met a representative from Vantara and the US lobbyist firm Checkmate Government Relations, founded by Ches McDowell, a hunting friend of Donald Trump Jr.

Before the meeting, internal briefing documents said Checkmate was “seeking to persuade the UK to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara and of Checkmate’s argument that responsibility for verifying legality sits primarily with exporting or re-exporting parties rather than with India as importer”.

During the meeting, the Vantara representative invited UK officials to visit the zoo – an invitation that was subsequently declined to ensure the impartiality of the UK’s Cites chair role.

After the meeting, a representative from another lobbying firm, Atlas Strategy, working on behalf of Vantara, approached the then secretary of state, Emma Reynolds, and then nature minister, Mary Creagh, also raising the prospect of a visit to the zoo.

When a department official asked for advice, a note was issued recommending that ministers did not engage formally with Vantara.

The government documents also reveal concerns about an application by Vantara to import falcons and falcon hybrids, after an unnamed organisation made a £50,000 “donation” to a breeder in the UK, whose identity was also not disclosed. British Cites officials classified the transaction as commercial, according to the documents. The falcons were not exported to India.

A spokesperson for Vantara said it had not imported falcons from the UK, nor had the zoo hired or paid a lobbying firm. It is understood that Checkmate and Altas Strategy were working pro bono for Vantara and not required to register with the Office of the Registrar of Consultant Lobbyists.

After recent scrutiny, Vantara has sought to “reset” its activities by voluntarily suspending animal imports for a year and hiring John Scanlon, a former Cites chief, to oversee its operations. In an interview with the Sunday Times this year, Ambani said he only brought animals to Vantara legally to ensure their welfare, mentioning a group of hippos he had saved from a hunting lodge in South Africa and jackass penguins saved from a shopping centre in Qatar.

“I think since I was a baby I have been taking care of animals. It’s always been a passion,” he said.

Vantara was used to host Ambani’s lavish pre-wedding celebrations in 2024, which were attended by celebrity guests and politicians. In September, Newcastle University awarded the oil heir a professorship of practice for “his work in conservation, biodiversity protection, animal welfare and environmental stewardship”, sparking a backlash from conservationists.

A Defra spokesperson said: “The UK has strict rules in place to protect endangered species and ensuring international wildlife trade is legal and sustainable, with every application assessed individually against internationally agreed and domestic requirements.”

Find more age of extinction coverage here, and follow the biodiversity reporters Rebecca Ratcliffe, Phoebe Weston and Patrick Greenfield in the Guardian app for more nature coverage.

#141. A Key Paris Agreement Goal Is Dead. What Now?

Leaders at COP31 are confronting a new reality as global warming enters the “unsafe zone.”

It was the climate movement’s unifying slogan for the past decade: “Keep 1.5 Alive.”

The tagline referred to the central ambition of the 2015 Paris climate agreement, in which almost every country pledged to try to limit global warming to 1.5 degrees Celsius.

Now, just 11 years after the target was set, 1.5 is effectively dead.

Scientists now say that without drastic efforts to reduce greenhouse gases, it is very likely that the planet will warm at least 2.6 degrees Celsius, or 4.7 degrees Fahrenheit, by the end of the century.

At COP31, the United Nations climate summit in Antalya, Turkey, diplomats who spent so long championing the 1.5 degree target lamented the abrupt setback.

“The world has no chance of holding to 1.5 degrees,” said Chris Bowen, Australia’s minister for climate change and energy, who is leading the negotiations.

Scientists for years have warned that allowing global average temperatures to rise 1.5 degrees Celsius, or 2.7 degrees Fahrenheit, above preindustrial levels would result in a profoundly more dangerous world. Extreme heat will become more common and more intense. Storms will become more powerful and destructive. Sea levels will rise. And poor countries will bear the brunt of the damage.

That scenario is already playing out around the world. In recent months, wildfires have raged from Canada to Indonesia. The collapse of a glacier in Nepal killed thousands and wiped away towns. Floods and mudslides devastated Brazil. And a European heat wave killed an estimated 50,000 people.
