#119. Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in Gaza kills surgeon and five others

Hospital director says 28 wounded in attack on one of few remaining medical facilities in the strip

An Israeli airstrike on Nasser hospital in southern Gaza killed six people, including a surgeon and a disabled man, and wounded 28 others, according to the hospital director.

The Israeli military carried out the attack on the hospital, in Khan Younis, early on Thursday saying in a statement it had struck a Hamas commander and other fighters there, without providing evidence.

The strike destroyed part of the surgery department, and caused extensive damage to several floors. Services were suspended in four departments: radiology, laboratories, surgical operations and inpatient surgical wards, according to Dr Ahmed al-Fara, hospital director.

The ceiling of one of the wards had caved in, while debris-covered beds lay next to medical instruments twisted from the force of the blast. In other rooms, piles of rubble and shattered concrete cluttered the floor and a hole was left in the floor, where the bomb penetrated the building.

Dr Ayman Abu Ubaid, a surgical specialist at Nasser medical complex, who was wounded in the attack, said: “The bombing caused thick smoke to fill the rooms and fires to break out, amid the groans of wounded and people’s screams. There were many casualties and blood everywhere … Most of us sustained injuries to our limbs, as well as shrapnel wounds in different parts of our bodies.”

Ubaid said the strike came at about 12.30am, without warning. “The patients in the department were all surgical patients waiting to undergo operations. The bombing happened at a time when they should have been asleep,” he said.

Among the dead was Rizk Abu Shaira, 52, a general surgeon who had featured in American Doctor, a documentary about doctors operating in Gaza.

“Dr Rizk was in the American Doctor documentary operating and serving his people. He lost his life doing that, he lost his life because he was doing that,” Dr Thaer Ahmad, who also featured in the documentary, posted on X.

Abu Shaira was killed alongside one of his patients, on whom he had operated.

Nasser hospital is one of the last functioning healthcare facilities in Gaza, after Israeli bombing made most of the strip’s facilities unusable. Last year Israel also struck the hospital, killing five journalists in a double-tap strike on a location often used by reporters for filming. In all, the hospital has been attacked seven times since the war on Gaza began.

The Israeli military has accused Hamas of using hospitals as command centres and to store weapons, an accusation the militant movement rejects. More than 1,700 healthcare workers have been killed in Gaza, while Doctors Without Borders (MSF) says Israel has detained hundreds of medics since 7 October 2023, with at least 95 still in custody.

About 90% of Gaza’s health infrastructure has been destroyed or damage by Israeli bombing, systematic destruction UN experts called “medicide”.

Attacking healthcare facilities and staff is illegal under international humanitarian law, though these facilities can lose their protected status if they are used for military purposes.

There is an increased need for healthcare during the winter months in Gaza, where rains have displaced people from their tents and increased communicable diseases in crowded encampments.

Already, Nasser hospital is lacking about 80% of its essential medicines and medical supplies, said al-Fara, its director.

One of those killed in Thursday’s strike was Seif Ghazi Abu Hatab, 29, who was admitted to Nasser hospital on Monday after a piece of debris fell on his back while he was helping clear rubble from a friend’s home. He was scheduled for surgery at 10am on Thursday, and was meant to receive further treatment abroad.

“Seif was a young man in the prime of his life. He wanted to live, rebuild his home and start a normal life, free from problems and suffering … Seif was killed after already losing two of his brothers and his father. His mother has now lost three of her sons,” said his cousin Tareq Abu Hatab, 37.

Israel launched the war in Gaza after the 7 October 2023 Hamas-led attack that killed about 1,200 people and took 251 hostage. At least 74,645 people have since been killed in Gaza and Israel has carried out a genocide in the strip, according to the UN independent international commission of inquiry on the occupied Palestinian territory.

Israel continues to carry out airstrikes in Gaza, killing more than 1,600 people since the October 2025 “ceasefire”, according to the Gaza ministry of health.

#118. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#115. NAZA: how and where to watch the Guardian’s acclaimed Gaza documentary

Film features testimony from 24 Israeli officials involved in surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians

NAZA will be released in full on the Guardian’s website for free in early December. Sign up here to be notified of the exact streaming date and to be sent a link to the film when it is live.

NAZA is currently screening in cinemas all over the world. It has been shown by cinemas in 47 countries and seen by more than 1.5 million people since its release last month, making it one of the most-watched documentary films with a theatrical release in 2026. The film has been offered to a number of distributors and cinemas in Israel but has not been screened there yet.

NAZA is a documentary featuring testimony from 24 Israeli military intelligence officers and soldiers involved in the surveillance and remote killing of Palestinian civilians during the war in Gaza. The film’s title comes from an Israeli military acronym used euphemistically to refer to civilians expected to be killed in a military attack.

The interviewees detail the methods used by the Israeli military in Gaza, including AI-based systems built to identify large numbers of potential Hamas operatives, who were then bombed in their homes at night, in the knowledge that the attacks would also kill their families.

Interviews were filmed at night on rooftops in Tel Aviv, on the condition of anonymity, because of the risks involved in speaking publicly. The appearance and voices of participants in the film have been digitally altered to disguise their identities.

The film was directed by the Oscar-winning Israeli directors Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor and produced by the Guardian. It builds on revelations first published by the Israeli-Palestinian magazine +972, the Hebrew-language news outlet Local Call and the Guardian newspaper between 2023 and 2025.

Abraham and Szor were two of the four co-directors – with the Palestinians Basel Adra and Hamdan Ballal – of No Other Land, which documents the destruction of Masafer Yatta, a village in the occupied West Bank targeted by Israeli forces. It won the Oscar for best documentary in 2025.

NAZA was co-produced by James Wilson and executive-produced by Jonathan Glazer, the team behind the Oscar-winning film The Zone of Interest.

NAZA has received widespread critical acclaim and is selling out in cinemas across the world. The New York Times says it is “tough to watch and impossible to ignore”, while New York Magazine describes it as “one of the most important films of our time”. In a five-star review, the Financial Times says that “journalism and film-making brilliantly entwine”. British Vogue calls NAZA a “brilliant and harrowing film”.

The film won the special jury prize at the Venice film festival in September, where its premiere had a 25-minute ovation – a festival record. In November, it won the best political documentary from the Critics Choice Association.

The film, its directors and their confidential sources have faced fierce attacks from the Israeli government and military. The prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, has ordered the attorney general and the head of the Shin Bet security service to reveal the identities of sources interviewed and prosecute them for causing “harm to Israel’s diplomatic and security interests”.

Netanyahu also threatened to strip Abraham and Szor of their citizenship, accusing them of treason. The Israeli military’s chief of staff, Eyal Zamir, said the military would “not allow soldiers to be made targets through lies and blood libels” and would use “every tool at our disposal” against the film.

Far-right groups have vowed to hound the film-makers on their return to Israel and picketed Szor’s family home. Some of the messages included explicit calls for violence, with one protester demanding the “death penalty for traitors”.

The Israeli military claimed there were “substantial errors” in the film and that it had relied on sources who had only a partial picture of the target approval process. It said that while AI was used to help identify targets in Gaza, final decisions on strikes, and the risk of civilian casualties, were signed off by officers.

In response, the team behind the film have revealed they spoke to more than 100 sources with knowledge of the military systems used in the war.

The Israeli government is promoting an online film project, called NAZA: The Real Story, which mimics the style of the genuine NAZA film and is hosted on a website called nazadocumentary.com. It is not the real NAZA documentary and has been misleading some viewers.

Initially, the rival film’s website falsely presented itself as a promotional page for NAZA itself, naming Abraham and Szor as the directors, and listing film festivals where the documentary was to be screened. The site now appears as a platform for the rival film, which has been promoted by Israeli embassies.

Ariel Averbuch, an Israeli attorney and army reservist who co-created NAZA: The Real Story, describes it as an “offensive move” to shift the world’s focus from allegations about the Israeli military’s killing of civilians.

Szor and Abraham have addressed the backlash to their documentary. “Our country is doing so much to hide these systems,” Szor said.

NAZA is in cinemas worldwide and will screen for free on the Guardian’s website from early December. Sign up here to be the first to watch it

#114. Nvidia Scandal Unleashes a Flood of AI-Generated Memes

The revelations surrounding Jensen Huang, who resigned from Nvidia over extramarital affairs, inspired social media posts using the very technology his company helped develop.

Jensen Huang and his leather-clad likeness had become synonymous with the technological giant he co-founded, Nvidia. They were so entwined that, this year, the company sold $178 sweaters with a pixel-art version of Mr. Huang on the front.

Now, in the wake of Mr. Huang’s resignation on Thursday after back-to-back cheating scandals, including an extramarital affair with a woman the U.S. government believes is a Chinese spy, the internet has taken ahold of his image to unleash a flood of memes.

In many cases, they are being made by A.I. enthusiasts who revered Mr. Huang.

Images of Mr. Huang have appeared in online parodies of music videos, Bollywood movies, American sitcoms and kiss-cams at Coldplay concerts — many generated using artificial intelligence, the technology that became widely available through the chips Nvidia produces.

The revelations about Mr. Huang, 63, one of the most powerful figures in the A.I. boom, are part of a long list of infidelity scandals that became memes.

Last year, the married chief executive of a tech start-up was caught on a kiss-cam at a Coldplay concert embracing the married but separated head of his company’s human resources department.

Mr. Huang’s image was superimposed on that viral moment by Peter Dedene, a Belgian software engineer. He said in a phone interview on Thursday that he was up late this week on his phone, scrolling the headlines about Nvidia.

#112. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#111. Lewis Hamilton says ‘Israel’s genocide in Gaza is appalling’ after Rafah visit

F1 driver urges Israel to let aid into Gaza

Trip involved tour of facilities with aid workers

Formula One driver Lewis Hamilton said on Wednesday that Israel must allow aid ⁠trucks into Gaza, a day after he visited the Rafah border crossing in Egypt.

Hamilton, a seven-time ⁠world champion, arrived ⁠in ​El Arish on Tuesday, where he was briefed on how aid is prepared and coordinated before it ⁠is sent into Gaza, as he toured the Rafah crossing and the Egyptian Red Crescent’s aid warehouses.

He has frequently posted on social media about children killed in Israeli airstrikes in Gaza and called for a ceasefire. A ceasefire agreement reached last year has halted major combat operations in Gaza, but the amount of aid reaching the territory continues to fall far short of the agreed-upon minimum of 600 trucks a day.

“The past ⁠three years of Israel’s genocide in Gaza is appalling. It’s an ​injustice for people to ‌be denied their freedom, ‌their safety and their dignity. No human being should be treated ‌as though their life matters less,” the Ferrari driver wrote on Instagram.

Israel strongly denies accusations of genocide and says its military campaign in Gaza targets Hamas militants.

Hamilton described seeing food trucks lined up and ready to go at the border that were ‌regularly held up or faced major delays.

“I saw walls stretching for miles, built to keep them in. Hamilton, 41, is an ​advocate for humanitarian causes. In 2021 he founded ​Mission 44, a ‌charity that supports ​young people ​and works to improve their access to education and employment.

“I think we need to make sure we’re pushing to free Congo, free Sudan, free Syria, free Palestine,” Hamilton said last month. “I think there’s a lot of peace that I’m hoping we get to at some stage.”

At the crossing on Tuesday, Hamilton greeted aid workers with “Assalamu alaikum” a widely used Arabic greeting meaning “peace be upon you”. He shook hands and briefly spoke to workers and officials.

#110. Paul Barril, French ‘Supergendarme’ Turned Mercenary, Has Died

After years in elite police units, he drew scrutiny from human rights groups for selling arms and providing other services to unsavory governments. He was 80.

Paul Barril’s offer to African dictators was straightforward: Your opponents will be subjected to surveillance and manipulation. Newspapers will be “infiltrated.” Plots against the state will be “neutralized.”

Plenty of heads of state took up the services he offered, including Omar Bongo in Gabon and Denis Sassou-Nguesso in Congo. Other clients went to Paris to do their shopping at his Security Action Store, which offered the latest in bulletproof vests or night-vision goggles, as Mr. Barril recounted in one of his six autobiographies, “The Elysee’s Secret Wars” (1996).

Mr. Barril was often described in the news media as a former French “supergendarme” for his longtime work in elite units. After he left the gendarmerie, or national police, in 1984, he became a security adviser and mercenary provider across the African continent and was implicated by human rights groups in the Rwandan genocide of 1994.

His death, on Oct. 20 at 80 at his home in Antibes, France, was announced by the French interior ministry. He had Parkinson’s disease.

By the time he founded his mercenary-security company, Secrets, a French acronym for Security Equipment Planning and Realization Company, in 1984, Mr. Barril was a well-chronicled public figure.

In the gendarmerie, which in France is under the military, he joined a new military police intervention unit, the GIGN, in 1974. The French news media chronicled Mr. Barril’s reputation as an ultrafit top cop, a military-grade swimmer and diver, a black belt in karate, a parachutist, a hostage liberator and a policeman working inside and outside the law. With the GIGN, he participated in the 1979 operation against Islamist militants who had seized the Grand Mosque in Mecca, taking dozens hostage.

#108. Two men found guilty of helping Iran try to ‘eliminate’ UK-based journalists

Mostafa Sepahvand and Farhad Javadi Manesh carried out surveillance in an effort to inflict violence on targets

Two men of Iranian origin who were hired through social media as part of a plan to “eliminate” journalists in the UK have been found guilty of assisting Tehran’s intelligence services.

Mostafa Sepahvand, 41, and Farhad Javadi Manesh, 46, carried out surveillance and reconnaissance between August 2024 and March last year in an attempt to inflict violence on “subjects” in the UK.

These subjects included two journalists for the London-based Persian broadcaster Iran International, Mojtaba Pourmohsen and Kambiz Hosseini, and a founder member of UK Lawyers for Israel, Jonathan Turner.

The defendants visited addresses linked to their targets, took photos and videos and used platforms such as ChatGPT to research them online. They appear to be part of a growing new tactic used by hostile states in which “proxies” are hired to work for foreign security forces, with often little idea who they are targeting or why.

Last month the Norwegian teenager Johannes Natland, 19, was jailed for life after he agreed to carry out a hit in Huddersfield for the Foxtrot Network, a Swedish organised crime group backed by the Iranian regime.

The Iranian men came to the UK illegally between 2016 and 2022 – Sepahvand in the back of a lorry and Manesh in a small boat.

Sepahvand used the AI platform ChatGPT to find the home address of Pourmohsen, who worked for the London-based Persian language broadcaster Iran International. He hoped to be paid £150,000 to carry out a violent attack and help to “finish off” Pourmohsen.

Sepahvand chatted on WhatsApp to his handler, an Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps commander called Amin, as he spied on Pourmohsen in December 2024, Woolwich crown court heard.

Amin wrote: “These are new pictures of the guy/bloke.”

Referring to a picture, Amin said: “And that dot is their office?”

Sepahvand said: “Love you brother, I’ll go there tomorrow.”

The pair also discussed surveillance of the Iranian satirist Kambiz Hosseini, who eventually had to leave home because of the threats made against him.

Bill Emlyn Jones KC, prosecuting, said Iran and its rulers did not and could tolerate dissent. “One organisation critical to the regime, and therefore very much in its sights, is Iran International, a news organisation which has come under particular attack because of its explicit support for opposition groups and its editorial stance, which is anti-Islamic Republic of Iran.

“Eliminating hostile, critical or dissident actors overseas is a staple policy of the Islamic republic. Together, the defendants were engaged in what is very obviously a form of spying,” Jones said.

Hosseini, 51, had to leave home and stop work as a standup comedian as a result of the threats he received. He was sent a message with a picture of his car saying the next time he started the engine it would blow up. Hosseini’s family in Iran also received threats, and his family had to leave the country.

The court heard Sepahvand had been convicted of theft in Iran and was drawn into the plan to “eliminate” dissidents in the hope of reducing his criminal record.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

He told his Iranian handlers he was prepared to escalate from surveillance to violence, the court heard.

He hoped to receive a final payment of £150,000 for “finishing the job”, a reference to killing one of the journalists, the court was told. “If the person [the target] is inside, they pay the £150,000, and I’ll finish him off,” said Sepahvand in a voice note.

Sepahvand claimed his family was being threatened and that he never intended to go through with the hit. He also denied plotting to kill Turner.

Manesh claimed he took a picture of the Iran International site because it was “a beautiful building” and did not know they were working with the Iranian intelligence services.

Earlier this year, Roman Lavrynovych, 22, and Stanislav Carpiuc, 27, were jailed at the Old Bailey for seven and two years respectively for arson attacks on property connected to Keir Starmer. An anonymous Russian-speaking figure named El Money had recruited Lavrynovych and Carpiuc on Telegram.

Last year, six Britons were jailed for their parts in a Russian-ordered arson attack on a London warehouse providing aid to Ukraine. The ringleader, Dylan Earl, was recruited by the Wagner group, a mercenary organisation that acts on behalf of the Russian state.

Sepahvand was convicted by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct. Manesh was convicted of assisting a foreign intelligence service, but cleared of preparatory conduct.

Shapoor Noori, 57, of Ealing, was cleared by the jury of assisting a foreign intelligence service and preparatory conduct.

Sepahvand, Manesh and Noori had claimed asylum in the UK and received temporary leave to remain. None of them had regular full-time employment.

It is understood they met by chance and were then directed by Sepahvand and his contacts in Iran. There appeared to be no evidence of any wider plot.

Mrs Justice Cheema-Grubb remanded Sepahvand and Manesh before sentencing at the Old Bailey on 17 December.

#105. Howard Lutnick Took Softer Line on Nvidia as Calls for Tougher Controls Grew

Critics say Howard Lutnick, the commerce secretary, and his department have shielded Nvidia as concerns have grown over A.I. chip sales to firms like Megaspeed.

Howard Lutnick has frequently clashed with top officials over how to regulate sales of advanced technology to Asia, particularly Nvidia’s chip sales in the region.Credit...Doug Mills/The New York Times

By Ana SwansonTripp MickleMaggie Haberman and Jonathan Swan

Ana Swanson, Maggie Haberman and Jonathan Swan reported from Washington, and Tripp Mickle from San Francisco.

There is broad agreement among most senior Trump administration officials that the United States should more aggressively tighten controls on Nvidia’s advanced chips to keep China from using them to catch up in the race to dominate artificial intelligence.

Then there is Howard Lutnick, who runs the Commerce Department.

Over the past year, Mr. Lutnick has frequently clashed with top officials over how to regulate sales of advanced technology, particularly Nvidia’s chip sales to Asia. That has drawn accusations from others in the administration and in Congress that the Commerce Department has become too cozy with Nvidia and its co-founder, Jensen Huang, and failed to adequately police the firm’s chip sales to China, in particular.

The issue has exploded into public view in recent weeks with the revelations from The New York Times that Nvidia’s chief executive, Jensen Huang, was in a relationship with a woman believed by U.S. intelligence agencies to be a Chinese spy. The woman, Alice Huang, was a top executive at a Singapore-based company, Megaspeed, that is suspected of smuggling Nvidia chips to China. On Thursday, Mr. Huang announced that he was stepping down from the company, sending the company’s shares tumbling.

The astonishing developments have thrust Mr. Lutnick into an uncomfortable spotlight and raised questions about whether he went too far in protecting Nvidia’s interests. And it has focused attention on whether the influence of Mr. Huang, an ally of President Trump’s and among the most high-profile and successful of American business leaders, kept the United States from moving faster to clamp down on sales of Nvidia chips to companies like Megaspeed.

The stakes in the A.I. race between the United States and China are high. China hawks warn that allowing sales of advanced chips to that country is speeding its A.I. development and giving it an economic and military edge. Those who support the sales say they help U.S. technology maintain global leadership, while deterring China from developing rival products. As the head of a sprawling department that has control over enforcing restrictions on technology sales, Mr. Lutnick is best positioned to single-handedly shape whether China has access to American technology, including Nvidia’s.

Within the Commerce Department, Nvidia is treated differently than other firms, current and former officials say, with its issues given high priority and sent through specific channels.

A controversial bill approved in October in the lower house of Italy’s parliament includes measures that allow healthcare workers who were struck off their professional registers during the pandemic for serious violations connected to their anti-vaccination beliefs – for example, promoting treatments that lacked a scientific basis – to apply to be reinstated.

The bill is being championed by the prime minister Giorgia Meloni’s Brothers of Italy party and is strongly backed by its far-right coalition partner, the League.

But with allies in Forza Italia split, its approval in the lower house hinged on support from National Future (FN), a rival far-right force led by the former army general Roberto Vannacci, whose emergence is shaping the political landscape before general elections next year. Vannacci has voiced opposition to vaccine mandates, and his rhetoric frequently targets the vaccine-sceptic audience.

The measure still needs to clear the senate, with a vote scheduled in January. But its progress has alarmed medical experts, less because of the number of healthcare workers involved – it is believed to be very small – and more because of the risk of further eroding trust in vaccines.

Nearly one in two Italian adults are hesitant about vaccination, according to a report by the department of public health and paediatric sciences at the University of Turin published in the Lancet Regional Health Europe in February. The report, which sampled 50,000 adults across Italy, found the ambivalence was driven more by misinformation and lack of institutional trust than by fears over vaccine safety.

#101. In French Election, Centrists Eye a Judo Star and a Central Banker

Centrists want alternatives to a matchup between the far right and far left in the 2027 presidential election. Teddy Riner, the judo champion, is in, while Christine Lagarde is less likely.

When Teddy Riner, a judo champion and one of France’s most popular athletes, announced this week that he would run for president, it was less a game changer than a welcome diversion. For all of his appeal, Mr. Riner, 37, is unlikely to win the race to replace President Emmanuel Macron next spring.

Yet his quixotic candidacy reveals a hard truth about France’s presidential campaign. Five months before voters go to the polls, many are eager, even desperate, for a fresh face to upend a race that looks increasingly as if it’s on a glide path to the election of France’s first far-right government since World War II.

The main far-right candidate, Marine Le Pen, has held a commanding lead over all her rivals for months — a lead that has only been reinforced by weeks of protests, marred by violence, that have shaken France. The most prominent center-right candidate, Édouard Philippe, has faded in the polls as the unrest has sharpened the appeal of far-right calls for law and order and left-wing calls for social justice.

Another beneficiary of the upheaval is a far-left firebrand, Jean-Luc Mélenchon, who now appears to be the likeliest candidate to advance into a second round against Ms. Le Pen in France’s two-round system. As things currently stand, French voters would be confronted with a choice between two poles of the political spectrum.

Centrist voters are “afraid of being confronted with a duel between Marine Le Pen and Jean-Luc Mélenchon,” said Jean-Daniel Lévy, deputy director of Toluna France, a polling firm. These voters, he said, are an “orphan electorate,” lacking an inspiring candidate, and are “still looking for someone.”

Raphaël LLorca, an analyst at the Jean-Jaurès Foundation, a left-leaning research group in Paris, said the search for a white knight showed “the panic of the centrist bloc.” He added, “It is clear that the election is slipping away from them.”

#100. Kenya discharges last Ebola patient in major milestone towards virus-free status

Kenya has reported no new confirmed cases since 20 October 2026

Kenya has discharged its last Ebola patient - the seventh to recover from the virus - marking a major milestone in efforts to contain the disease in the East African nation, which now begins a 42-day countdown to officially being declared Ebola-free.

It had confirmed its first ever case of Ebola early in October when a Kenyan businessman died within days of arriving in the capital, Nairobi.

He had contracted the virus in the Democratic Republic of Congo, where more than 5,000 people have died in an ongoing outbreak that began in May. His mother also subsequently died.

Kenya's health minister said 135 identified contacts of the infected patients had also completed their 21 days in isolation.

"Today with the discharge of the last patient we can confirm and tell the world that seven patients have recovered - and all contacts have completed their follow-up monitoring," Aden Duale said.

The Africa Centres for Disease Control and Prevention (Africa CDC) described it as an important achievement in the regional response to the outbreak.

"This is an encouraging moment for Kenya and for Africa. We must sustain surveillance and vigilance until the outbreak is officially declared over," Africa CDC chief Jean Kaseya posted on X.

What is Ebola and why is stopping the latest outbreak so difficult?

Kenya has reported no new confirmed cases since 20 October.

All the Kenyan cases were linked to the man who had travelled from DR Congo through Uganda, with health officials reporting no evidence of wider community transmission.

Those who have recovered following treatment included four healthcare workers.

Five patients were discharged on 3 November, followed by a sixth three days later, before the final patient was cleared on Wednesday.

Under World Health Organization (WHO) guidelines, 42 days must pass after the last patient tests negative and is discharged, or the last Ebola victim is safely buried, before an outbreak can be declared over.

The Ebola virus spreads through contact with infected bodily fluids such as blood or vomit. The incubation period ranges from two to 21 days, meaning those exposed to the virus may not become ill for several weeks.

Duale said the government would maintain heightened surveillance at border crossings, airports, health facilities and communities throughout the countdown.

Emergency response teams will remain on alert across the country, while the health authorities will continue monitoring travellers arriving from affected countries.

Kenyans returning from Ebola-affected areas are required to complete electronic health declarations before travelling, undergo screening on arrival and spend 21 days in quarantine under the supervision of health teams.

The minister also urged countries that had imposed travel and visa restrictions to reconsider them - adding that Kenya had no intention of serving as a quarantine centre for another country.

In May, a Kenyan court suspended US plans to open an Ebola quarantine facility for American citizens.

Kenya has maintained that there is no need to restrict domestic travel, trade or public gatherings.

The outbreak has nevertheless disrupted international travel, with some Gulf countries introducing restrictions affecting travellers from Kenya and other African countries.

Saudi Arabia stopped issuing visas to Kenyan nationals in late October, prompting concerns among those seeking employment there.

Meanwhile, the United Arab Emirates (UAE) has announced that it is lifting its suspension on issuing new visas to Ugandan nationals, easing restrictions introduced in June.

But Ugandans will have to spend at least 21 days outside of Uganda before being allowed to travel to the UAE.

The UAE is also allowing certain students, diplomatic passport holders and Ugandan embassy employees to enter under approved health procedures.

Uganda, which shares a long and busy border with DR Congo, was among the countries first affected but declared its own Ebola outbreak over on 27 August.

The current outbreak in DR Congo has been caused by the Bundibugyo species of Ebola, which is relatively rare, with no approved vaccine or treatment - though trials are under way.

#99. Four grizzly bears killed in Canada after fatal attack on mother and daughter

Between 10,000 and 13,000 grizzly bears are estimated to live in British Columbia (file image)

Four grizzly bears have been killed in connection with a fatal attack on a mother and daughter in the Canadian province of British Columbia.

Jennifer Balcarras, 48, and her daughter Selah Reed, 20, died earlier this month after going on an afternoon walk in a trail area in Bella Coola.

The British Columbia Conservation Office Service (BCCOS) launched an investigation into their deaths and forensic testing has since confirmed a sow and her three cubs were responsible for the attack.

All four were destroyed "in accordance with provincial human-wildlife conflict response guidelines" to protect the public, BCCOS Inspector Jeff Tyre said in an update on Tuesday.

Mother and daughter die after suspected grizzly bear attack in Canada

"Evidence from the investigation as well as animal DNA was analysed and conclusively linked to a sow and its three offspring," he added.

The attack "likely involved predatory behaviour", which Tyre said was "very rare".

He added that more than a dozen conservation officers took part in the investigation over the past week, amid "difficult terrain and weather".

Balcarras and Reed were "two peas in a pod" who "got along really, really well" and shared many hobbies, Balcarras' brother, Josh, told CBC.

"When Jenny hiked, Selah was right there beside her," he said.

Tyre said DNA testing also confirmed the four bears were not involved in a 2025 attack on a school group also in the Bella Coola area.

The incident in November of last year saw 11 people injured, two of them critically, while the bear responsible was never found.

"Today, we have learned from the BC Conservation Officer Service members that they have captured and euthanized the bears involved in the recent fatal attack," said Samuel Schooner, Elected Chief of the Nuxalk Nation, in a statement.

"We would like to thank the BC Conservation Officer Service for their dedication and hard work to resolve this incident. This attack has been deeply re-triggering for our community members as we continue to recover from last year's attack. Our thoughts remain with the victims' families."

#97. Norway follows Finland in barring Russian athletes from entering country over Ukraine war

Move by Norway ‘a further tightening’ of current rules

Russians ‘have no business’ in Finland, says minister

Norway’s government announced that it would prevent Russian and Belarusian professional athletes from coming to Norway to compete, hours after neighbouring Finland announced a similar move.

The government said it intended to prevent athletes from these countries coming to Norway as long as the war in Ukraine continues. In a statement, it noted that while Norway already had strict rules for visas for Russian and Belarusian citizens, recent “relaxations introduced by international sports federations” meant that more athletes and support staff from the two countries may come to the Scandinavian nation to compete.

In a statement, Astri Aas-Hansen, the minister of justice and public security, said: “Norway already has a restrictive visa practice for Russian and Belarusian citizens, but is now further tightening who is allowed to travel here.”

To this end, the government said it would instruct immigration authorities and police to “to refuse entry to Russian and Belarusian professional athletes and accompanying support staff … if they come to Norway to take part in sporting competitions”. The changes would enter into force “shortly”, the government said.

The statement said that Russian elite sport had close ties to Russian authorities and could therefore not be “viewed independently” from the war in Ukraine.

Norway is preparing to host World Cup in cross-country skiing in December in the town of Trondheim, but this decision means that Russian and Belarusian athletes could be blocked from competing despite having been cleared by the International Ski and Snowboard Federation (FIS).

FIS has permitted Russian and Belarusian athletes to compete under certain conditions, such as not displaying any country symbols and signing a declaration of neutrality.

The Norwegian move follows a similar decision by Nordic neighbour Finland, which earlier on Thursday announced it would block visas for Russian athletes.

Finland also said it will restrict entry for Russian athletes because of the war in Ukraine, potentially affecting a number of winter sports. The move follows a backlash in Finland to Russia being reinstated for the ice hockey world championships.

“As long as Russia’s war of aggression continues, Russian athletes have no business in Finland,” said Elina Valtonen, the minister for foreign affairs. “Their entry into the country would be a threat to Finland’s international relations. The same is true of other short-term work under visas that is not essential to Finland’s national interests.”

The Finnish foreign ministry said the measure would affect Russians competing under neutral status as well as those competing for national teams.

Finland hosts a figure skating grand prix next week and a cross-country ski World Cup this month. Both sports allow Russians to compete as “neutral athletes”. One major event to be affected is the figure skating world championships in March. Russian skaters are expected to be among the top medal contenders.

The International Skating Union (ISU) said it “has taken note of the statement today by the Finnish government related to Russian nationals entering the country and the possible consequential impact on sporting events, including the ISU figure skating world championships due to be held in Tampere, Finland, and is studying its implications”.

“ISU believes that athletes should not be punished for the actions of their governments in a world where, sadly, many conflicts are ongoing.” The ISU added it was “seeking clarifications” from skating officials in Finland.

The change takes effect from Monday, so should not affect this week’s NHL games in Helsinki between the Seattle Kraken and Carolina Hurricanes or scheduled ski World Cup slalom races in Levi on Saturday and Sunday.

Finland is among a group of European teams to have vowed not to play against Russia at the ice hockey world championships. The men’s championships are scheduled for Germany, which is weighing up whether to go ahead with hosting.

In the longer term, Finland is due to host the European track and field championships in 2030. World Athletics, the sport’s governing body, maintains a blanket ban on Russian participation, though that is facing a legal challenge.

→ Baca artikel asli

#96. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#95. Kenya Starts Countdown to End of Ebola Outbreak

The milestone is further evidence that African countries have mostly succeeded in preventing community transmission and isolating the small number of cases that have come in from Congo.

Kenya began a countdown on Wednesday to declare the end of its Ebola outbreak.

The final patient who had tested positive for the virus was discharged from the hospital on Wednesday. And the Kenyan cabinet secretary for health, Aden Duale, said at a news conference that the last 11 people being monitored for Ebola symptoms had also been discharged after completing a 21-day quarantine period.

“For the first time since this outbreak began, Kenya has no patient under treatment and no contact in quarantine,” he said.

The milestone is further evidence that countries have succeeded in containing the small number of cases, most coming in from the Democratic Republic of Congo, where the outbreak continues to spread.

Since Kenya announced its first-ever Ebola case on Oct. 6, two people have died and nine have tested positive in the country. The initial case was a Kenyan man who returned from Congo and infected others before the virus was diagnosed and he was put in isolation. He died days later.

The Kenyan authorities say that all of those who subsequently became ill were in quarantine before they developed symptoms and became infectious. As a result, there have been no instances of what experts call community transmission, when a person passes on the virus outside a medical setting.

Kenya’s outbreak will be officially declared over on Dec. 23, or 42 days from when the last case was recorded, if there are no further positive Ebola tests, the health secretary said. In all, 135 people have been monitored for signs of the virus.

The current Ebola outbreak has killed more than 5,300 people since it was first declared a global health emergency by the World Health Organization in May. But so far, the fear that the epidemic might spread unchecked across Africa has not materialized.

Uganda has recorded 20 cases and three deaths, but the outbreak was declared over in the country by August. The authorities in Nigeria last month identified a case in the capital, Abuja.

Individuals with Ebola have since been medically evacuated to several European countries. Two Americans who worked for humanitarian organizations involved in the Ebola response have also tested positive. Both were treated outside of the United States and have recovered.

Several countries have instituted travel restrictions to prevent the spread of the virus. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention instituted a requirement in November that anyone traveling by air into the United States must sign a document confirming that they have not been in Congo in the past 21 days before boarding their flight.

Kenya has also announced new rules for its citizens returning from Congo, including 21 days of quarantine and monitoring, Mr. Duale said. He gave no details of whether the quarantine would take place at home or in a medical facility.

Matthew Mpoke Bigg is the East Africa bureau chief for The New York Times, based in Nairobi, Kenya.

#94. US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent relocated after alleged Iranian threats

The severity and nature of the threat against Scott Bessent remains unclear

US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent has been moved from his Washington DC home to a military base after Iran-linked threats to his life, according to US media reports.

CBS, the BBC's US partner, said the relocation came after a photo of Bessent's home was published in the Wall Street Journal.

Bessent has been at the forefront of efforts to isolate Iran economically through sanctions and a wider economic blockade.

While the exact nature of the threats against Bessent remain unclear, US intelligence has warned that Iran has "substantially increased" efforts to target Americans, including US officials.

"After the Wall Street Journal published an article featuring an online photo that allowed the location of the secretary's Georgetown home to be geo-located, security concerns began to increase," a source told CBS.

Georgetown is an affluent part of Washington that is home to a number of government officials and prominent residents.

Those security concerns, the source added, prompted authorities to move Bessent to a military base in the area.

In a statement, a Treasury Department spokesperson said only that Bessent "will not be deterred" by threats and "remains committed" to the US effort to pressure Iran's economy.

The BBC has contacted the White House for comment.

In September, Director of National Intelligence Jay Clayton warned that Iran has "substantially increased" efforts to target Americans.

The New York Times has reported that the Iranian effort includes the targeting of current and former US officials. One source told the newspaper this has "reached a more advanced stage".

Other US officials reportedly on Iran's list of potential targets include former Secretary of State Mike Pompeo, former National Security Advisor John Bolton and Mark Milley, the former chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff.

Milley and Bolton's security teams were taken away by the Trump administration shortly after he returned to office last year.

Bessent is one of a number of administration officials that live on military bases. The White House's new chief of staff, Stephen Miller, was moved to a base in late 2025 - before the conflict with Iran began.

Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth and Secretary of State Marco Rubio both live on military bases, as did former Attorney General Pam Bondi and former intelligence chief Tulsi Gabbard.

The US and Israel launched an air campaign against Iran in February, killing its leader and prompting the regime in Tehran to retaliate with strikes on US allies across the region and the effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz, through which around a fifth of the world's oil and gas usually travels.

In June, the US and Iran reached a preliminary agreement to end the war and reopen the strait, but it collapsed within weeks after Iranian attacks on shipping resumed and the US reinstated a blockade targeting Iranian ports.

#92. ‘Win-win’ deal with EU could be done, Andy Burnham says

Prime minister says reset could be a deal ‘like the Brexit people thought they were voting for in 2016’

Andy Burnham has said he believes a “win-win” deal could be done with the EU that could be “more like the Brexit that people thought they were voting for in 2016”.

The prime minister was meeting fellow leaders at the European Political Community summit in Dublin, ahead of a keenly anticipated EU-UK summit next week, where he hopes to bring Britain closer to Europe.

At the bilateral summit the EU and UK hope to sign deals on food and drink, emissions trading and a youth mobility scheme, but the question of tuition fees for European students remains a stumbling block.

Burnham said: “I believe there is a good deal to be done that could be a win-win for Britain and Europe.

“The deal is not done, and there’ll be some long nights ahead in the next seven days.”

He said the reset would be about whether we can “take a step back to closer working relationships with Europe”.

Burnham added: “We ended up with a far harder Brexit, I think, than people thought they were getting when they went to the polls.”

The EU has said the prized food and drink deal and emissions-trading agreement may not enter into force without a deal on tuition fees in which European students would pay domestic rates.

Burnham said it was a package deal, but would not be drawn on whether he accepted the link. “I’m going to be fighting hard for these benefits for Britain in the next seven days … they will make a real difference. If I can do anything to address the cost of living crisis, I should be doing it.”

He has previously said the food and drink deal, which will ease Brexit red tape, will add 0.4% to British GDP.

The prime minister also said David Cameron had been wrong to argue that Britain’s problems had “nothing to do with Brexit”, adding there would be “long nights ahead” to negotiate a reset with the EU before the summit.

Cameron, the prime minister who lost the 2016 Brexit referendum, told Bloomberg News that Burnham should not seek to bring Britain back into the EU, arguing that the UK’s problems had nothing to do with its exit from the EU.

Asked about those remarks, Burnham said: “I think he is wrong to say that the low growth that we’ve had over the last decade has, quote, ‘nothing to do with Brexit’. I would say the evidence says otherwise.

“It feels like his intervention is more about unity in the Conservative party than it is about fighting for the British interests.”

Burnham has recently said he is open to rejoining the single market, customs union or to “go all the way” by rejoining the EU. He said a deeper relationship would be the outcome of future talks “and it will be in the outcome of that negotiation that then we will take a decision as a cabinet, on what, if anything, comes next”.

Earlier on Thursday, the EU foreign policy chief, Kaja Kallas, said she wanted to see Britain rejoin the EU, but warned that negotiations would not be easy after the “trauma” of the Brexit years.

She said: “I would very much like to see Britain back in Europe, but, of course, we also have in the European Union, a lot of, I would say, trauma from Britain leaving, so the negotiations will not be that easy.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Burnham is expected to make the EU reset the centrepiece of his white-tie speech at the lord mayor’s banquet on Monday night – though it will range widely across foreign policy challenges.

No 10 sources said the speech would be focused on the upcoming Brussels summit – and look ahead to the next stage in 2027 – rather than outline what a more permanent close relationship might look like.

Officials at the UK-EU summit will also discuss the agenda for the next round of the reset, expected to focus on a joint migration pact.

It could also target collaboration on law enforcement and borders, visas for touring artists, and mutual recognition of qualifications.

Ministry of Justice officials are also trying to water down an EU ultimatum on halting the deportation of foreign national offenders. “This is the classic wheeze that the EU always pulls at the last minute of negotiations,” one Whitehall source said.

The next week is expected to be fraught with last-minute negotiations. Key will be access to Made in Europe, which has the potential to lock British firms out of continental supply chains and public contracts if no deal is done.

Negotiations could be challenging despite the lure of the UK making a potential concession on tuition fees in exchange for inclusion in the Made in Europe legislation.

The EU has long said there cannot be a deal on Made in Europe at the summit, because it has yet to finalise its own position.

The joint communique for 2027 is expected to call for strengthening efforts between the UK and the most exposed member states to the small boats crisis in the Channel.

This will centre on both the EU’s Channel action plan, already in place since June this year, and a possible framework for a wider bespoke EU-UK migration plan.

The UK is believed to be pushing for access to the Eurodac database, which carries the fingerprints of asylum seekers, as well as to the Schengen Information System (SIS), which records details on fugitives, missing people and stolen goods.

The UK was the third biggest user of SIS before it lost access through Brexit.

#91. Putin takes hard line in US talks as Ukraine says Starlink disrupted again

Russian president says he would target any foreign troops sent to Ukraine and refuses to soften territorial demands

Vladimir Putin warned US negotiators that Russia would target any foreign troops deployed to Ukraine, in a series of hardline remarks that came as the Ukrainian military faced renewed disruptions to Elon Musk’s Starlink satellite service.

During the talks at the Kremlin with the US envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff on Thursday, the Russian president refused to soften his territorial demands, insisting on full control over four Ukrainian regions that Moscow claims to have annexed.

He also ruled out accepting the deployment of foreign peacekeeping troops in Ukraine, describing such a move as a “continuation of war”.

Putin’s comments appeared to severely dampen hopes that a peace deal could be within reach, after Volodymyr Zelenskyy said there was a “window of opportunity” to reach an agreement before the end of 2026.

The latest trip to Moscow by Witkoff and Kushner, who have made numerous visits to Russia, was partly aimed at securing European participation in future negotiations, according to two people familiar with the talks.

Speaking after the meeting, Witkoff confirmed that European representatives would join the next round of talks. “They’re our partners, and it’s important for them to be involved,” he told reporters as he boarded a plane leaving Moscow.

The US envoys will now travel to Dublin, Ireland, where they will meet Zelenskyy and debrief him on the Moscow meeting.

Andy Burnham, the British prime minister, said national security adviser Jonathan Powell would join the talks in Berlin.

Amid a flurry of diplomatic activity, including the recent talks in Abu Dhabi, the Kremlin appears to have succeeded in reframing negotiations over the war in Ukraine as part of a broader discussion about European security.

But Europe is wary of Russian attempts to focus discussions on a European security architecture, while Moscow continues to pound Ukraine with bombs and missiles.

The EU foreign policy chief, Kaja Kallas, told reporters: “We already have a European security architecture in place.” Kallas pointed to the cold war-era Helsinki accords and the Budapest memorandum, which guaranteed the territorial integrity of Ukraine. “The problem is that Russia is not keeping to those agreements, so right now the focus should be that Russia actually keeps to their obligations … and they don’t attack another country again.” Focusing on a European security architecture was a “distraction” without a ceasefire, she added.

Both Washington and Moscow have been urging European leaders to appoint a single representative to lead negotiations on their behalf.

“Once a representative is appointed, a chief negotiator authorised to speak on Europe’s behalf, there will be a four-way dialogue,” Putin said on Thursday.

EU leaders are due to discuss the issue of who speaks for Europe on Friday, but so far have declined to nominate one person to speak for the bloc. One EU official said the EU would be represented by its institutions when issues concerning the union were on the table, but played down any imminent appointment. They said: “Do I expect an agreement on a special representative? My answer is no, I don’t.”

Kallas suggested she – as the EU’s high representative for foreign and security policy – should represent the EU in talks, alongside António Costa, the president of the European Council.

More than 45 European leaders are meeting in Dublin for the European Political Community summit, a gathering that brings together nearly every country on the continent, apart from the leaders of Belarus and Russia.

The French president, Emmanuel Macron, played down the importance of having a single European negotiator, saying “the content determines the format”. “There is no more US support. So there [are] some mediations, fine,” he said, referring to the process led by Kushner and Witkoff. “But at the end of the day we will be the one to define and decide for our future security as Europeans.”

The German chancellor, Friedrich Merz, said it was first necessary to get Putin to the negotiating table before deciding on a European envoy. Merz said: “It’s not about individuals. What matters is that Putin is prepared to hold talks.”

Meanwhile, Zelenskyy accused Musk’s Starlink satellite service of damaging Ukraine’s ability to defend itself against Russian drone attacks, after Ukrainian military officials reported new restrictions on the system.

According to Zelenskyy, Starshield, a military version of Starlink funded by the Pentagon, has for several days been subject to speed limits of 100mph (161km/h) along the frontline and in Russian-occupied territory.

Similar restrictions last month prompted confusion over who had ordered them, with Musk and the Trump administration pointing fingers at each other before the service was partly restored.

The latest restrictions had made it harder for Ukrainian forces to intercept Russia’s increasingly sophisticated jet-powered drones, Zelenskyy said, accusing the service of “helping the Russians kill more Ukrainians and carry out more accurate strikes against Ukraine”.

Kyiv has contacted Washington to seek an explanation for the disruption, which Zelenskyy said appeared to be deliberate rather than a technical fault.

#90. Missing 11-month-old baby rescued after being buried alive in a crate in South Carolina

Watch: County Sheriff describes how buried baby was found

An 11-month-old baby has been rescued in South Carolina after officials say he was buried alive in a crate by his father.

Koah Sharpe had been missing since Monday when he was taken by his father, Michael Comer, 25, according to the Lexington County Sheriff's Department. Comer was arrested on a burglary charge on Monday evening but his son was not with him.

The baby was found on Tuesday "buried alive" and "clinging to life" not far from his father's house, Lexington County Sheriff Jay Koon said at a news conference. It is thought he had been there for 24 hours.

The child is now in hospital receiving care, Koon said, adding he was hopeful he would make a full recovery.

Police are still deciding what further charges to bring against the father.

The search for baby Koah began on Monday after his father took him from his mother's house.

Comer's mother was watching the child and had left her son alone with him briefly, Koon said, when he left abruptly with Koah.

Officials allege the baby's father buried him alive in a dog crate

Comer was seen travelling to multiple locations without the baby and was later arrested in Lexington County without Koah. A search party of 100 police and fire department officials were then sent to search for the child, the sheriff said.

He added that when they asked Comer where his son was, he "did not have much to say" about his whereabouts.

Koon said that rescuers searched the woods near Comer's house and saw some "freshly disturbed dirt". As they began to dig, they heard a baby crying - something the sheriff described as "the most joyful sound you ever heard".

"The race was on. Everybody was getting down there trying to get him out," Koon added.

He said the crate was wrapped in a towel that probably provided enough air for Koah to survive for as long as he did, while the cold weather kept him from getting too hot.

"The crate was, probably, I'm guessing about a two-foot [61cm] by two-foot dog crate. So, I think, once that crate hit the ground, he was probably up at least a foot from the top of that crate to the top of the ground," Koon explained.

Of the parents' relationship, the sheriff said there were "obviously some issues". Comer had previously pleaded guilty to domestic violence charges, court records show.

Comer's mother, Patricia Comer, told NBC News that Koah's mother had full custody of him but allowed her to supervise while her son watched him. She said her son had watched the baby before unsupervised without any problems.

"Honestly, I'm his mother, and I never expected this from him," Patricia Comer said.
