#89. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.

#87. Nvidia Scandal Unveils a Fierce Spy War Raging Behind Trump and Xi’s Diplomacy

That the United States suspected Nvidia’s leader was a Chinese spy target underscores the deep distrust between the two powers, even as their leaders try to stabilize ties.

As President Trump and China’s leader, Xi Jinping, prepare to meet next week to extend a fragile trade truce, a fresh espionage scandal has shown that behind their diplomacy is a fierce intelligence rivalry.

At its center is Jensen Huang, the Nvidia leader and an influential ally of Mr. Trump’s, who has acknowledged having an affair with a Chinese businesswoman whom U.S. intelligence officials suspect of spying for Beijing. China has denied the espionage allegation.

Under Mr. Xi, the Chinese Communist Party has built a more sophisticated and assertive spy service that regularly highlights the importance of technological security and of threats posed by the United States. At the same time, the Central Intelligence Agency has said that Chinese companies are a legitimate target for espionage in areas like artificial intelligence, semiconductors and biotechnology.

Nvidia’s computer chips power the global A.I. boom and sit at the center of Washington’s efforts to keep that technology out of China’s hands. That makes Mr. Huang, who has lobbied Mr. Trump to ease restrictions on the export of advanced chips to China, a prime target, analysts say.

“It is not at all surprising, given the centrality of Nvidia to U.S.-China tech competition, and the importance of tech competition in the overall U.S.-China rivalry, that someone like him would be a top target for Chinese intelligence collection,” said Sheena Chestnut Greitens, an expert on Chinese security at the University of Texas at Austin.

#86. More than 60% of Germany’s female soldiers report harassment or abuse

Defence minister says extent of sexual misconduct found by study is ‘unacceptable’, as military tries to attract more recruits

Germany’s defence minister has promised a “zero tolerance” approach to sexual abuse in the armed forces after a study found soldiers, particularly women, faced extensive harassment within the ranks.

The first comprehensive study of its kind into the Bundeswehr, conducted by the Centre for Military History and Social Sciences in Potsdam and released this week, comes as the military is trying to attract new recruits in response to global threats.

It found that more than 60% of female soldiers had reported experiencing some form of sexual harassment or abuse while on duty, ranging from sexually explicit remarks and degrading rituals to sexual violence.

Nearly 40% of the 4,000 women who responded to the survey said they had been addressed with suggestive comments and sexualised remarks, while 15% reported being harassed with mobile phone pictures taken against their will and in some cases altered using AI.

Just over 8% reported having been physically attacked, and 2% said they had been the victims of sexual violence.

Around 19% of male ‌soldiers had experienced a similar form of sexual misconduct, with non-binary or gay soldiers disproportionately affected, the study found.

Boris Pistorius, the defence minister, said the figures were “unacceptable”, in particular for a military that is trying to considerably increase its numbers. The Bundeswehr has pumped billions into boosting both personnel and equipment. Military bosses have set a target of 260,000 active-duty troops and 200,000 reservists by 2035, in the hope of avoiding the introduction of conscription.

Pistorius said he was struck by the finding that women in units with a higher proportion of other women were less frequently affected, which he said “argues in favour of recruiting more women into the Bundeswehr”.

He said he had been advised by some colleagues to ditch the study, over fears it would sully the reputation of the military. To do so, he said, would be akin to “turning a blind eye”, which in the long term would do more damage to the Bundeswehr. The study would be repeated every year, he added.

“Zero tolerance applies to sexual harassment within the Bundeswehr,” the minister said.

Pistorius expressed concern over a high level of reluctance to report incidents. About a third of those surveyed said they did not or would not report incidents because of a lack of trust in what their superiors would do.

The most pressing stories and debates for Europeans – from identity to economics to the environment

Before the study results were made public, Pistorius set up a hotline this month, meant to serve military personnel, which is to be staffed around the clock, and is called HELP.

In future, Pistorius said, those who made complaints should receive more information about the status of their proceedings, after victims said they were often left in the dark, but expected to continue serving.

The ministry said that, of 45,000 active or former members of the armed forces contacted to participate in the survey, 18% responded. It said its analysis showed that the figures were nevertheless representative.

#84. Schools in Italy, Scotland and Northern Ireland hit by threats in suspected bomb hoax

These children had to leave their primary school in Rome as a result of one of the threats sent on Wednesday

Some 6,300 schools across Italy and more than 700 in Scotland and Northern Ireland have received bomb threats, according to local authorities.

In Italy a number of buildings were evacuated as a precaution but Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni said checks so far had not indicated any evidence of "concrete danger".

"These reports appear rather linked to a strategy intended to cause alarm and concern," she said on Facebook.

Scotland's education secretary Mairi McAllan said there was no risk to the public and specialist cyber-crime officers were working to identify the source of the messages. In Northern Ireland education officials told schools to remain open unless otherwise advised by police.

It is unclear whether the incidents are connected.

"As a parent and as a constituency MSP I understand just how concerning this will be for parents, for pupils, for carers and for school staff," McAllan said.

Scottish police had earlier said that the warnings were "immediately assessed" as hoaxes. However some local authorities confirmed that some schools had been evacuated.

"Our advice is that there is no requirement to evacuate schools or keep children away from education based solely on the content of the emails," Police Scotland said.

Italian officials said a single email appeared to have been sent on Wednesday to the inboxes of 6,300 out of a total of 7,476 state schools, and private schools were unaffected.

The message, said to be from a certain "Mikhail Andropov, leader of Vilayat di Misoginistan", caused alarm in many schools.

The BBC has seen the email sent to Scottish schools which appeared to have similar wording, and also referenced the name Mikhail Andropov, although as leader of a "Red Owl" project.

Italy's Education Minister Giuseppe Valditara said the incidents involving Italian schools were probably a "cyber-attack, not a genuine risk", adding that there appeared to be a clear strategy behind the rash of threats in across Europe targeting flights, supermarkets and even hospitals.

Italian airports along with airlines elsewhere in Europe were hit by a series of bomb hoaxes last week that prompted a series of scares, evacuations and widespread disruption.

Although a number of threats were claimed by an extremist misogynistic group, it later denied any link to the false warnings.

No risk to public from hoax school bomb threats, says government minister

Police treating threatening email sent to more than 300 schools as 'hoax'

On Wednesday one Milan headteacher, Anna Bertato, told Italian media she had checked the email address of the sender online: "It was already classified as spam by a student at a university in Taiwan."

Another Milan headteacher, Massimo Nunzio Barrella, told Adnkronos news agency that he had immediately realised the email was spam when it arrived in his inbox at 08:59 and he decided not to evacuate his school and reassured parents who phoned in.

"The text seemed almost comical," said Barrella, "it was like a message from Martians: 'We have come to annihilate you.' I even had a bit of a laugh."

Although many schools were initially evacuated on Wednesday, the alarm was quickly lifted, and pupils were allowed to return to class. Some parents decided to take their children home for the day, but the majority of schools in Milan did not send their pupils home.

Some 280 school head teachers had already received threats in recent days, according to Italian reports.

In a statement, the head of the central Umbria region Stefania Proietti said "checks conducted so far in Umbrian schools have proved negative, and law enforcement is working rapidly and carefully to verify each report".

#82. Kidnapper, 74, escapes from house arrest in Argentina by abseiling from balcony

Leonardo Bertulazzi was first arrested in Buenos Aires in 2002

A 74-year-old Italian man who was found guilty of a kidnapping carried out by a far-left militant group in the 1970s has escaped while under house arrest in Argentina and is on the run.

Leonardo Bertulazzi used a harness to abseil down from a fifth-floor balcony in the capital, Buenos Aires.

His escape was not immediately registered because he had tampered with his electronic ankle monitor so that it continued to transmit body temperature measurements even after he had prised it open and absconded.

Bertulazzi already spent decades as a fugitive after he was sentenced in absentia in Italy for the 1977 kidnapping of Italian naval engineer Pietro Costa.

He was awaiting extradition to Italy and was being monitored by the Argentine ministry of security through an ankle tag which registers the wearer's body temperature and movements.

Officials said that they had become suspicious when they noticed that the tag had remained stationary.

When they entered the fifth-floor apartment in the centre of Buenos Aires, Bertulazzi was gone, but the ankle monitor remained.

Wary of Bertulazzi's past with the Marxist guerilla group the Red Brigades and his bomb-making skills, police called in explosives experts in case it had been rigged to detonate, according to Argentine newspaper ClarÃ­n.

However, the experts found that while Bertulazzi had tampered with the device, he had done so to prevent the alarm from sounding once he removed it.

Italian media say that his extradition to Italy had been imminent after his last appeal was rejected.

Bertulazzi was first arrested after using a false passport to cross into Argentina from neighbouring Chile in 2002.

However, he was granted refugee status in 2004 and lived freely in the South American country until his re-arrest in 2024, when the current right-wing president, Javier Milei, took office.

Milei's government said at the time that "Bertulazzi is responsible for crimes that undermined democratic values and the lives of many victims".

He was a member of the Red Brigades, which kidnapped and killed a number of state officials in the 1970s and '80s.

Bertulazzi was part of the Red Brigades' Genoa section that kidnapped Pietro Costa on the street and then held him for 81 days before receiving a big ransom.

The money it made from the kidnapping was then used to buy a flat in Rome, which was used in 1978 for the Red Brigades' most notorious crime, the kidnap and murder of Italian former Prime Minister, Aldo Moro.

Moro was abducted when his car was ambushed on the way to the opening of parliament. He was held in the flat for 54 days before he was shot and his body abandoned in the boot of a Renault car in the centre of Rome.

#81. Alexander Darchiev, Russia’s Ambassador to the U.S., Dies at 66

The death of Alexander Darchiev was announced by Russia’s foreign ministry. He was appointed to his post in Washington in 2025, as relations between the Kremlin and the White House warmed.

Russia’s ambassador to the United States, Alexander Darchiev, who served during a rapprochement between the Kremlin and Washington, has died, the Russian foreign ministry said Wednesday. He was 66.

Mr. Darchiev died in Moscow of a “chronic illness,” Maria Zakharova, spokeswoman for the ministry, said in a statement published by Tass, the Russian state news agency.

“We have lost an outstanding diplomat, an experienced leader, a kind colleague and a mentor,” the ministry said in its statement. It described Mr. Darchiev as a “distinguished expert on the United States” who had “served in one of the most demanding areas of Russian foreign policy.”

Mr. Darchiev had been in the diplomatic service since 1992, and he was appointed as ambassador to the United States by President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia in March 2025.

His appointment came as President Trump sought to broker a cease-fire agreement to halt Russia’s war against Ukraine, which began in 2022. Mr. Darchiev’s term coincided with a thaw in relations between the Kremlin and Washington as U.S. policy shifted from staunch support for Ukraine under the Biden administration to pressure on Kyiv to make a deal with Russia.

In February last year, Mr. Darchiev led a Russian delegation meeting with U.S. officials in Istanbul about the operations of the two nations’ diplomatic missions. At the time he was director of the North Atlantic Department, a section of the Russian foreign ministry that oversees relations with Canada, Ireland, Britain and the United States.

“The dialogue on irritants has been ongoing, and certain improvements have been achieved,” Mr. Darchiev said last year in an interview with the Russian newspaper Kommersant in which he discussed frictions between the United States and Russia.

But he said the United States was tying discussions on certain issues, like Russian access to seized diplomatic properties in the United States, “with a settlement in Ukraine that suits its interests,” which he objected to.

Talks on ending the war in Ukraine, which had been stalled for much of this year, resumed this week in Abu Dhabi, the United Arab Emirates, after a push by the Trump administration that began in September. The discussions are not expected to yield fruit anytime soon.

It is not clear if Mr. Darchiev was involved in those talks.

Mr. Darchiev, who was born in 1960, graduated from Moscow State University in 1983. Information about his survivors was not immediately available.

Mr. Darchiev’s appointment to Washington as ambassador to the United States was not his first stint in the country or the region. He served as Russia’s ambassador to Canada from 2014 to 2020 and worked at the Russian Embassy in the United States from 2005 to 2010, according to a biography on the embassy’s website.

In the intervening years, Mr. Darchiev directed the North America Department and, briefly, the foreign ministry’s North Atlantic department, before serving in what proved to be his final role.

Ephrat Livni is a Times reporter covering news around the world. She is based in New York.

#80. US disrupts North Korea's $400 million crypto scam, FBI says

The US has seized servers linked to a North Korean scam that used fake video meetings and deepfakes to steal more than $400 million in crypto, and has moved to seize $1.2 billion that was taken from Binance.

The United States Justice Department said Thursday it had seized cloud infrastructure used by North Korean hackers in a video-call scam that has stolen more than $400 million (€361 million) in cryptocurrency over 18 months.

It also said it had court approval to seize or restrain more than $1.2 billion taken in the recent Binance hack.

The Federal Bureau of Investigation calls the scam "FaceStealer."

According to the Justice Department, hackers sent meeting invitations from accounts taken over from earlier victims. Once a target joins, the hackers record their webcam video and play a prerecorded clip of a previous victim the target knows, so trusted contacts appear to be present. The FBI says hackers are also starting to use AI-generated deepfakes of well-known crypto figures.

The hackers then stage a fake technical error and offer a link to "fix" it, which installs malware, the department said. In some cases, they waited months, or over a year, before stealing funds.

After that, they use the victim's contacts to find new targets.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Most targets work in crypto and other internet-related fields, and the hackers are increasingly going after open-source software developers, apparently to enable supply-chain attacks, the department said.

Under a federal warrant, the FBI seized more than 900 webcam videos of victims.

"If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough," said Patrick Grandy, who heads the FBI's Los Angeles field office.

The FBI ties FaceStealer to a hacking cluster known as Sapphire Sleet or UNC1069. It is separate from the North Korean military hackers blamed for the Binance theft.

In the Binance case, the FBI said the group known as TraderTraitor stole more than $6 billion (5.4 billion euros) on or around October 29. AP put the figure at about $6.3 billion (5.7 billion euros) and called it the biggest crypto heist on record. US agencies and private firms have since frozen more than $2.7 billion (2.44 billion euros), the department said.

Authorities usually file a forfeiture claim in court, after which a judge decides who has a rightful claim to the recovered funds, AP reported.

"North Korea's government bankrolls its rogue regime and illicit weapons program through cryptocurrency theft,"  said M. Scott Proctor, the department's principal deputy assistant attorney general for national security.

North Korea has denied involvement in the Binance hack.

#79. Chips, creek water and Take Me Home, Country Roads: how an Australian teenager survived six nights in the bush

William, 17, says ‘nights were pretty cold’ and he ‘honestly didn’t think’ he’d survive

A teenage boy who survived almost a week lost in dense Australian bushland sang John Denver’s Take Me Home, Country Roads as he held out hope of rescue on long nights alone in the wilderness.

William, whose surname has been withheld, was dropped off by his mother for the weekend to camp with his friend in Victoria’s east last Friday.

The 17-year-old was reported missing at about 4pm that day after failing to return from a trip to retrieve further camping equipment. He was wearing only shorts and work boots and had left his phone, food supplies and water behind.

After an extensive operation of more than 100 people, including the police dog and rescue squads, the police air wing found William on Thursday morning lying down in a creek near Walhalla, 130km east of Melbourne.

He lost his way after taking a wrong turn on a track, and later slipped and fell down a cliff.

He told Seven’s Sunrise program John Denver’s country classic, which repeats the line “take me home, to the place I belong”, was a childhood favourite of his that brought him solace as he braved harsh conditions and loneliness.

“It’s a good childhood song of mine. It brings me great comfort,” he said from his hospital bed in Traralgon on Friday morning. “I started doing it one night, made me feel OK, so I just kept doing it.”

William shared an emotional reunion with his mother, Elizabeth Barkley, soon after being rescued. She told reporters on Thursday between tears that she was “over the moon” and he was “never leaving the house again”.

William told Sunrise their warm embrace was the “best moment of my life”.

“I think she’s going to stand true with that comment,” he said with a laugh. “She’s giving me a firm look from across the room at the moment, so not expecting to go out any time soon.”

William said he was “doing great” after enjoying a “great sleep” in a “comfy bed” on Thursday night and “finally getting some food into me”.

“I’m over the moon to be safe,” he said. “The nights were pretty cold. I think a couple of times it was raining. Hot days, so I got a bit sunburnt, pretty treacherous surroundings.

“I honestly didn’t think I’d make it six days.”

After seeing the helicopter flying overhead, William, who was sheltering on a rock under towels and heavily sunburnt, gave a wave and fist-bump before being airlifted to safety. He said he was feeling “pure euphoria”.

“Just happiness and gratefulness that a moment that I thought might never happen, has happened, and I am finally going to see my mum again,” he said.

“There were a few times were I’m thinking ‘geez, it’s getting dark out here, I don’t know if they’re going to see me’.”

William got by on a packet of chicken-flavoured chips, which he rationed over several days. He said he also drank water from the creek that he was sheltering by.

“Last night I had dinner … and I had some Macca’s, which was good, so I got plenty of food in,” he said. “I’m super grateful for all the paramedics and nurses, search and rescue … everyone that was involved.”

Pilot Dave Wylie told reporters it had been an incredibly challenging search for crews and it was lucky William was sheltering in an open area.

“A lot of the areas are so thick that you can’t actually see the forest floor so even if someone was there, potentially you could fly right past them,” he said.

“If he wasn’t in the open area like he was, we absolutely could have flown right past him without seeing him.”

William was being transported to the Alfred hospital in Melbourne for further observation and testing by specialists, after suffering from dehydration. He was in a stable condition on Friday afternoon.

Barkley told Nine’s A Current Affair she was in awe as to how well her son had handled days alone in the bush.

“I just couldn’t believe he survived that long,” she said. “But he’s a footballer. He’s tough, he’s very, very fit.

Barkley said William had missed a turn on a track and became lost on Friday and curled up under some bushes when it got dark.

“I had instinct … I knew he was still alive,” she said of the following days. “I knew in my heart … And I was just praying, and praying, and praying.”

#78. Famed ship SS United States deliberately sunk off Florida coast to become a reef

Watch: Moment historic ship sinks off the coast of Florida

A ship that was once the fastest in the world and is longer than the Titanic has intentionally been sunk off the coast of Florida, turning into the world's largest artificial ocean reef.

The SS United States spent nearly 30 years docked in Philadelphia before it was moved to Florida, where it will become a home for sealife and an underwater attraction for marine enthusiasts.

Activists unsuccessfully fought for the historic liner - which crossed the Atlantic Ocean hundreds of times - to be preserved as a museum.

Instead, after being purchased by a Florida county, it was added to the dozens of artificial reefs off the coast, with a land-based museum detailing the ship's history also planned.

Before its retirement, the SS United States was known for carrying royalty and celebrities between the US and Europe, beginning in 1952 and ending in 1969.

A young Bill Clinton, before he was president, used the ship to cross the Atlantic. Former presidents Harry Truman, Dwight Eisenhower and John Kennedy also travelled on the vessel.

The ship was moved from Philadelphia to Alabama last year for cleaning and remediation work.

On Monday, it took its final journey from Alabama to the coast of Destin, Florida, where it was sunk on Wednesday in a precision operation without explosives.

This archival video shows the SS United States in 2025 as it starts its move to Florida on its way to become the world's largest artificial reef.

Stars including Marlon Brando, Coco Chanel, Duke Ellington, Salvador Dali, Walt Disney, Judy Garland and Cary Grant also boarded the ship during its sailing lifetime.

Before its purchase by the municipal Florida government, a number of proposals that would have seen the ship live a second life as a museum or a floating hotel failed.

Instead, it sat at various docks with various owners as legal battles played out.

The SS United States Conservancy group repeatedly attempted to keep the ship above water, but was unsuccessful.

Conservancy President Susan Gibbs - whose grandfather helped design the liner - said she was glad that the story of the SS United States would live on.

"[The ship] has always been this unabashedly American symbol that brings so many people together," Gibbs told CBS News, the BBC's US partner.

#77. Indonesia's vice-president resigns amid signs ruling alliance is fraying

President Prabowo (left) said he was sorry to lose Gibran Rakabuming Raka (right), who has been mired in a growing controversy over his high school credentials

Indonesia's Vice-President Gibran Rakabuming Raka tendered his resignation to parliament on Thursday, triggering the process of finding his replacement amid growing turmoil for the government.

Gibran had been facing impeachment calls after a Supreme Court ruling that a high-school document used during his 2024 election campaign was invalid.

His departure comes amid a faltering political alliance between his father, ex-president Joko Widodo, and the incumbent President Prabowo Subianto.

Standing alongside Gibran, Prabowo told reporters he had accepted his resignation with "a heavy heart". Upper house lawmakers will have 60 days to fill the vacancy from two candidates nominated by the president.

"[Gibran] has demonstrated a chivalrous attitude, a strong sense of responsibility, and the qualities of a statesman," Prabowo said, adding that the resignation was "for the benefit of the nation, the state, and the people of Indonesia".

"He certainly believes that he completed his education through a proper and structured process. However, we must understand that educational systems abroad differ from those in Indonesia," the president said.

Gibran has long faced controversy over whether his high school qualifications met legal requirements to run for office.

Having studied in Singapore and Australia, he had used an Indonesian equivalence certificate to demonstrate his educational credentials.

Indonesian law requires vice-presidents to have completed senior secondary school or its equivalent, but the Supreme Court found no evidence to prove Gibran had graduated.

"No evidence was found to show Gibran had a foreign diploma or certificate that could serve as the basis for equivalency with a senior high school or equivalent qualification," it said.

It also considered a Constitutional Court ruling from earlier in October which came to the same conclusion.

The Supreme Court ruling prompted more than 100 lawmakers to support a parliamentary initiative that could have paved the way for proceedings to impeach Gibran.

Gibran previously argued that undertaking university education was sufficient proof that he had completed senior high school.

On Tuesday, he asked for the chance to challenge the ruling and be given the opportunity to prove the legitimacy of his education credentials, State Secretariat Prasetyo Hadi said.

Gibran's ascension to Prabowo's ticket in the 2024 election was part of an alliance formed between his father, outgoing president Joko Widodo - known as Jokowi - and Prabowo, his erstwhile bitter rival.

Gibran was 36 at the time, and only became eligible for candidacy after a controversial ruling by the Constitutional Court, which exempted him from the minimum age requirement of 40.

But tensions between Jokowi and Prabowo appear to have flared.

Since taking the presidency, Prabowo has shown little interest in pushing ahead with Jokowi's legacy project: the construction of Nusantara, a new capital. Instead, he has focused much of the state's resources on his own flagship programme to provide free school meals across the country.

In a series of personnel reshuffles over the past year, Prabowo has also shifted many Jokowi loyalists into new roles, including long-serving finance minister Sri Mulyani Indrawati.

Last week, authorities arrested several senior officials, including presidential chief of staff Listyo Sigit Prabowo - no relation to Prabowo - on suspicions of wiretapping the president.

Prabowo sacked Listyo a day later, in what many pointed to as a sign of a crumbling alliance: Listyo, who formerly served as the national police chief, is widely considered a long-time ally of Jokowi.

Within the government, allegiances seem to be shifting. On Monday, three members of Prabowo's cabinet collectively resigned from the party led by Kaesang Pangarep, Jokowi's youngest son, citing their desire to focus on their duties in Prabowo's administration.

But neither man has confirmed the rift. Speaking to reporters on Thursday, Jokowi said he still supported Prabowo's leadership.

Jokowi also said Gibran's resignation was part of "political dynamics" and he had told his son "the position was temporary".

All this is unfolding at a particularly turbulent period in Prabowo's presidency. The free school meals programme is struggling for funding, parts of Indonesia have recently battled the country's worst wildfires in a decade, and the national stock market faces crisis amid allegations of widespread market manipulation.

Across the country, students and activists have staged demonstrations to protest against the government's handling of a range of issues, including the environmental crisis and corruption.

#76. ‘If I wanted to plan a murder’: Beau Lamarre-Condon tells court what he would have done

Former police officer tells court ‘it was never a plan to murder anybody’ as prosecution repeatedly suggests to him he planned to kill Jesse Baird

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

Beau Lamarre-Condon has told a Sydney court he never planned to murder anybody and if he did, he reiterated he would use stolen vehicles and a gun not linked to him.

But the prosecutor responded: “No one’s saying it was a good plan, that’s why you’re sitting here.”

The former police officer is accused of fatally shooting Jesse Baird and his new partner Luke Davies with his police service weapon in Baird’s Paddington home in Sydney on 19 February 2024.

He has pleaded not guilty to two counts of murder and one charge of aggravated break and enter.

Sat in the witness box for the seventh day, Lamarre-Condon was asked about driving his mother to pick up a hire car under her name on the evening before the alleged murders.

As part of his cross-examination, crown prosecutor Brett Hatfield SC repeatedly suggested to Lamarre-Condon that he was planning to murder Baird.

But the accused told the New South Wales supreme court on Friday “it was never a plan to murder anybody”.

“You wouldn’t bring a vehicle linked to your mum for a murder,” Lamarre-Condon told the jury.

“If I wanted to a plan a murder I would have two stolen vehicles ready to go, I would have a gun that’s not linked to me. I know exactly how to do it, I work with youth gangs.”

Hatfield told Lamarre-Condon: “No one’s saying it was a good plan that’s why you’re sitting here, isn’t it?”

The former officer maintained he and his mother spoke in the car about his suicidal ideation and he had turned off his phone because he did not want to talk about work or personal things in the vicinity of the device.

Earlier on Friday, Lamarre-Condon was asked about purchasing a sim card under the name “Bob Ballard” on 15 January 2024, about a month before the shootings.

He told the jury in his double murder trial it had “nothing to do with Jesse Baird”.

He was attempting to compile statements about “bullying from a certain sergeant” but was worried about speaking publicly on the issue, Lamarre-Condon said.

“I was wanting to go and compile all these statements and go to the media,” he said.

“I didn’t want to communicate at all from my phone because you can become a target … there would be repercussions.”

Under cross-examination by Hatfield, Lamarre-Condon agreed he was using the new sim card to contact former and current officers.

But asked why his phone records showed he only contacted one police officer on this sim, Lamarre-Condon said it only revealed his normal phone calls, telling the court: “You can’t see what I did on Signal.”

The prosecutor said he could have used encrypted messaging app Signal on his normal phone, but Lamarre-Condon claimed police could make him unlock his own device and view his Signal records if he was investigated for talking to the media.

Hatfield also questioned why Lamarre-Condon had Davies’s contact details on his phone.

The former officer claimed Davies’s details were on his Instagram.

“I went and blocked numerous Instagram [accounts] from my own Instagram because of this incident,” he said, referring to the shootings.

“I even blocked Jesse’s family members from my account. I didn’t want any of that.”

He deactivated his social media accounts soon after.

The trial before Justice Natalie Adams continues.

#75. Workers in Spain Stage a General Strike for the First Time in 14 Years

Workers in a wide range of industries walked off the job to protest rising rents and stagnant wages, which have become central issues in the campaign.

Spanish workers staged a general strike on Wednesday for the first time in 14 years, protesting the rising rents and stagnant wages that have become central issues in the national election to be held this month.

Unions said millions of workers participated in the walkout, while hundreds of thousands of Spaniards joined rallies in Madrid, Barcelona, Valencia and other cities in the evening.

Early on Wednesday, protesters blocked roads in Madrid, set up picket lines outside major factories in Catalonia and disrupted transportation across Spain.

Police officers wielding shields and batons forced hundreds of demonstrators away from the Gran Vía, the central thoroughfare in Madrid, which rally goers had blocked. The anger of protesters in the capital was directed largely at “rentistas” — a Spanish term for people who live off rental income.

Later in the day, during a march through central Madrid that drew 120,000 protesters according to the government, demonstrators chanted in a festive mood, “One jump, two jumps, whoever doesn’t jump is a rentista.”

Clashes with the police erupted in Barcelona, where a group of masked protesters broke away from the crowd after the demonstration ended, according to local news media. The group smashed several shop windows, threw pyrotechnic devices at police officers and tried unsuccessfully to storm an Apple Store, the reports said.

#74. Australian teen missing in bush for six days rescued alive

Moment teen rescued after six days in Australian bush

A teenager missing for six days in dense Australian bushland has been found alive.

William, whose surname has not been released, disappeared last Friday while on a camping trip with a friend in bushland about 180km (112 miles) east of Melbourne, Victoria.

Extremely cold overnight temperatures had left authorities concerned for the 17-year-old's welfare. Police dogs, divers and helicopters had been involved in the search.

He was located on Thursday morning and reunited with his relieved family, who said he was uninjured but "grounded for life".

Rescuers winched William to safety, and fed him a doughnut and a bottle of water, before he was taken to hospital for a check up.

The boy's mother, Elizabeth Barkley, told reporters the teenager had "only a few scratches" and seemed fit and healthy.

"He's doing fine, everything seems to be fine, he's in perfect health," she added.

"He just stayed in one spot and waited to be rescued and I'm just over the moon he's alive."

Joking, she added: "He's never leaving the house again."

Police have said officers were emotional to have found the teen alive.

"It doesn't happen all the time," Victoria Police Acting Inspector Jason Benbow said. "I'm sure he's got a story to tell us soon, which we're very keen to hear," he added.

#73. Microsoft struggles to fix huge outage affecting Outlook and Teams

Company has ruled out a cyber attack but says fixes to help thousands of affected users have not been successful

Microsoft 365, Outlook, Teams and other apps were disrupted for thousands of users for more than nine hours on Thursday and Friday, with Microsoft scrambling to find a solution after initial fixes failed to bring services back online.

A Microsoft spokesperson said the company “had no indication of a cyber-attack”.

Outage website Down Detector showed a spike in Microsoft 365 problems around 1pm ET, with nearly 130,000 reports of issues in 74 countries affecting access to Word, Excel, PowerPoint, Outlook and Teams as well as enterprise services such as SharePoint and OneDrive. AI assistant Copilot was also affected.

Microsoft has suggested the outage may have been caused by issues with the settings applied to servers responsible for keeping Microsoft 365 up and running. Over the nine hours the outage had been going at the time of reporting, the company had attempted a number of solutions to bring back services, including rolling back updates and limiting the traffic flowing to affected servers.

When fixes had been deployed, Microsoft noted that those servers became unavailable again once traffic had returned.

As of 3.30am GMT on Friday, Microsoft said its Exchange services – the back end to Outlook email services – were being most affected, with flow-on effects to other products that rely on those services. Teams chats and meetings were less affected.

Microsoft’s executive vice-president of Copilot, Charles Lamanna, posted on X the company would share more information when it becomes available. “We regret that today’s outages caused disruptions for our customers, and we are working to address the issue,” he said.

“We will continue to share more information as soon as we have it, and for now I can confirm that there is no indication this is the result of malicious or cyber activity.”

Earlier, Microsoft said that a portion of its infrastructure was “experiencing elevated load” – which it said was “likely contributing to the connectivity errors”. The company determined a number of servers were “unexpectedly unavailable” due to a problem that caused them to fail during automated checks.

Posting on X, Microsoft said it was “investigating reports of users experiencing issues accessing Exchange Online mailboxes through multiple connection methods”.

Earlier the company had said the servers it had restored had remained stable so ‌far and that it was shifting traffic to alternate routes wherever possible while streamlining the process to bring the remaining affected servers back ​online.

“As capacity is restored, users may experience a gradual recovery as their clients reconnect,” the company said in a statement.

The Microsoft outage also hit connectors used by ⁠OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s AI ⁠services.

OpenAI said it was investigating an issue with Microsoft connectors in which new connections may be broken and tools were not working as ⁠expected. Anthropic said it had identified an issue with an upstream provider ​affecting its Microsoft 365 ​connector.

Microsoft last month reported an ​outage of its Azure cloud platform that affected ​suite of productivity software, ‌and a range ​of industries worldwide.

As the company worked to restart and validate affected servers, it said it was in parallel “assessing whether reverting the small number of recent configuration updates we identified could help accelerate recovery”.

Mark Gregory, associate professor in the School of Engineering at RMIT University, said the outage appears to be affecting the network layer in Microsoft’s systems, and was potentially similar to the configuration issue which brought down Optus’s network in 2023.

He said when some of the servers have configuration issues, it means customers are forced to share a smaller pool of servers to access the cloud services.

“If you lose part of that pool, then all of the users are trying to access the remaining servers, and that overloads them. And then they go down.

“And so you end up with a cascading problem. Then it takes time to find the underlying problem, in this case, a misconfiguration in the network layer.”

Gregory said it showed companies should invest more in testing environments for configuration changes before deployment in the live network, and said businesses reliant on cloud services such as Microsoft 365 should have backup options.

“From a global perspective, there is someone somewhere that’s that’s been badly affected by this and you end up with lost productivity,” he said.

“They’re being left high and dry now … it then becomes important to have an alternative.

“Many people would keep a Gmail account, if their Microsoft goes down, they use Gmail. If Gmail goes down, they use Microsoft.”

Earlier, the company said it had “successfully restored” problems related to Microsoft Teams in North America, although some users continued to report connectivity issues.

#72. Clashes erupt in Barcelona after thousands join Spain general strike

Watch: Protesters and police clash in Barcelona

Clashes have broken out between groups of masked men and police in Barcelona after tens of thousands of people took part in Spain's first general strike for 14 years, called by the country's main unions over housing and the high cost of living.

Some 120,000 people joined a demonstration in the capital Madrid and more than 50,000 turned out in Barcelona, where footage showed masked men setting fire to rubbish bins and smashing shop windows as police fired tear gas.

Transport and other public services across Spain faced serious disruption.

Spaniards are due to vote in parliamentary elections on 29 November.

The head of the big UGT labour union, Pepe Ãlvarez, said the strike was a success with "massive" participation, while Unai Sordo of the CC OO union said turnout was "very significant."

There was a heavy police presence in the centre of Barcelona on Wednesday evening, with officers wearing helmets and riot shields.

Some of the disturbances took place in the Passeig de GrÃ cia, close to the iconic PlaÃ§a de Catalunya in the centre of the city.

Small fires burned in the middle of the streets as bins were set alight. Local media reported that a group of people fired fireworks towards police and looted shops including the Lego store. Protesters were also seen trying to enter the Apple Store.

The BBC spoke to a woman who attended the protests in Barcelona who said it was mostly very peaceful and "joyful". She said she was there to protest about the housing costs and that "there were a lot of children and old people there."

She was in the area of Las Ramblas in the evening and saw fires in the streets. "The fires were everywhere, it was very intense," she said.

Earlier in the day there was major disruption around Madrid and Barcelona, with traffic halted on several main roads.

The interior ministry said dozens of people were arrested for a variety of incidents and Spanish reports said estate agencies had seen attacks on their offices.

Police said they had made dozens of arrests

"We've completely brought the city to a standstill," a protester in Madrid told Reuters news agency, adding there was widespread support for the general strike. "Everyone's out there, despite the rainy weather; everyone's out on the streets."

Another protester, 37-year-old teacher Olga Domingo, said she was protesting because "the housing situation is outrageous. The current cost of living compared to wages is completely unsustainable".

The strike was triggered by widespread protests over affordable housing and rising living costs, after the eviction of 87-year-old Maricarmen Abascal from a flat in Madrid where she had lived for seven decades. She died in hospital weeks later.

Rental prices in the capital have risen in recent years. The monthly cost of a 50 sq m apartment in Madrid was an average of â¬1,170 ($1,300; Â£1,000) in October, according to property portal Idealista, while Spain's average gross monthly salary is about â¬2,460.

That amounts to nearly half the average gross salary, well above the 30% level of affordability often recommended by housing experts and public institutions.

Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez called snap elections last month after parliament failed to approve his plan for housing reform.

Not since 2012, under the conservative government of Mariano Rajoy, has Spain held general strikes.

And while previous strikes have been problematic for the government in power, this one is different. SÃ¡nchez and his ministers have even encouraged workers to down tools and demand affordable housing.

"Your eight-hour day. Your pension. Your public healthcareâ¦ These were collective conquests," he wrote on social media, praising those who took part in previous strikes.

The eviction of Maricarmen Abascal at the end of September "was the little fire" that sparked this protest, he told the BBC.

Ãlvarez knew the 87-year-old, who had faced a tripling of her rent under new landlords, and says he was deeply upset by her treatment.

He rents a small, one-bedroom flat with his girlfriend, a dental hygienist, in the city's southern Arganzuela disrict. The couple pay â¬1,000 between them, which means each spends about half of their salary on housing, even before other bills.

"It's absolutely crazyâ¦ with the food, with the electricity, with the water, with gas."

He sees no way of getting on the property ladder: "You have to collect an insane amount of money to be able to buy a house. So with these salaries, it's impossible."

With current rent and purchase prices, he believes buying somewhere is "completely impossible".

Gonzalo Ãlvarez, who was taking part in the general strike, says 50% of his salary goes towards rent

However, the vice-president of the CEOE, Spanish biggest business association, Miguel Garrido, criticised the strike, complaining that businesses had little to do with the price of housing.

"This is not a strike for labour-related reasons, but rather for political ones, and it is closely linked to the early calling of elections," he said.

Demonstrations were planned for some of Spain's biggest cities later in the day and the scale of support will be widely viewed as an indicator of public discontent ahead of the election.

Another reliable indicator of support for strikes is the level of demand for electricity with Spain's industrial sector.

Power usage in Spain fell by 7.5% during the early hours of Wednesday, according to data from operator Red ElÃ©ctrica. During the last general strike 14 years ago, equivalent consumption was down by more than 15%.

The general strike took place little more than two weeks ahead of national elections in Spain

#71. Seeking to Broaden Ukraine Talks, U.S. Urges Russia to Include Europe

As a round of negotiations ended in Abu Dhabi, President Trump’s envoys said they would go to Moscow as the Kremlin demands discussions on European security structures.

Reporting from Abu Dhabi, United Arab Emirates

The first round of Ukraine peace talks in nine months ended in Abu Dhabi on Wednesday without a Russian agreement to include Europe in negotiations that have extended, at Moscow’s insistence, to questions of huge consequence for European nations’ security.

The two days of talks between Ukrainian and Russian delegations in the United Arab Emirates were led by President Trump’s envoys, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff. On Thursday, the two Americans are scheduled to meet with President Vladimir V. Putin of Russia in Moscow and then with President Volodymyr Zelensky of Ukraine in Dublin.

The Americans said on Wednesday that they would work to nail down the details of what Mr. Kushner billed as the largest humanitarian swap of the war.

The envoys are also trying to secure agreement from Moscow to widen the talks to include Europeans, after negotiators in Abu Dhabi discussed Russia’s demands to overhaul European security structures.

Reiterating a longstanding European position, Ursula von der Leyen, president of the European Commission, the European Union’s executive arm, said this week that a cease-fire must come before such discussions. That sequencing would avoid negotiating at gunpoint and test Russian compliance with any agreements.

But Mr. Kushner and Mr. Witkoff, trying to find a path forward after nearly two years of fruitless peacemaking efforts, are pushing to grant Moscow its wish of negotiating the postwar security posture in Europe alongside discussions about a cease-fire and territory in Ukraine.

#68. ‘Looks like we’ll have to talk to people’: Australia sent back to time before email with major Microsoft outage

Government services and schools impacted by Outlook and Teams issue as company says fix not ‘taking as expected’

Microsoft struggles to fix huge outage affecting Outlook and Teams

Australian schools, courts and government services have been hurled back in time to how business used to be done after a global Microsoft outage caused emails and online meetings to go dark.

Australians began reporting issues with Microsoft 365 – which includes Outlook and Teams – just before 5am AEST on Friday, according to Down Detector. The problem appeared to peak at 9.22am with more than 5,500 reports of problems, including trouble opening and sending emails, before reports began to dip.

A Microsoft spokesperson said the company “had no indication of a cyber-attack” and suggested the issue could be related to a configuration change to its servers that was causing some to become overloaded and taken offline.

In an update to its website on Friday afternoon, the company said efforts to fix the issue were not “taking as expected”.

Service NSW, the one-stop shop agency for residents, drivers, and business owners to access government services, encouraged customers to delay their visits to its service centres. The agency put in place a priority callback system for those unable to complete urgent transactions during the outage.

“Some transactions in Service Centres and Call Centres are unavailable at this time,” it said. “Customers are encouraged to delay their visit to a Service Centre and to complete transactions online, where possible.”

Earlier in the morning, the federal home affairs minister, Tony Burke, appeared at a press conference with the Australian federal police commissioner, Krissy Barrett, and the Asio director general, Mike Burgess. Both said their systems had been affected by the outage, but they provided no further details.

Burgess said the spy agency’s systems “operate on multiple levels”, adding that they had been affected “on one level, yes, but not completely”.

The Australian Medical Association (AMA) said it had received reports that some hospitals and health clinics were experiencing clerical issues, but there had been no impacts to patient care.

“We’ve had some facilities return to using paper for scripts because authentication to get into lots of prescribing software requires multifactor verification, which requires you to be able to access your Microsoft-supported email,” the AMA president, Dr Michael Bonning, said.

“We don’t use any of these things for direct patient care. It’s always the authorisation in systems, so we’ve always got workarounds for that.”

Schoolbytes, a cloud-based administration platform used by schools in NSW, was “temporarily” unable to access emails due to the outage.

A text sent to a parent regarding the platforms outage said: “If you emailed the school after 3.15pm yesterday, or need to communicate with us regarding absences or changes to routines please call the office …”

Courts in South Australia and Victoria reported that the outage was affecting its email system. Victoria’s county court said its scheduled hearings were expected to go ahead as planned.

On social media, people shared how the outage was affecting their workday.

One Brisbane-based person posted on LinkedIn: “It’s all SMS and phone calls today … looks like we will have to talk to people while Microsoft sort out their email issues!”

On Reddit, some users reported that while Outlook emails were down they could still access Microsoft’s workplace messaging platform, Teams.

“They could have taken Teams out with them so I’d be able to … just sit here and do absolutely nothing,” one Reddit user wrote.

Some services remained unaffected by the outage. The two major supermarkets – Coles and Woolworths – were operating as normal inside their stores. However, Coles said its internal email system has been affected.

The National Broadband Network said it was monitoring the outage, but remained unaffected.

The National Cyber Security Coordinator warned cybercriminals often seek to take advantage of the “uncertainty around these types of disruptions” with scam emails, texts, calls or social media messages.

“I encourage every Australian to remain vigilant and be cautious of unsolicited contact. Do not click on unexpected links, open attachments, or provide personal information. If you’re unsure, hang up and call back,” the coordinator wrote on social media.

Mark Gregory, an associate professor in the School of Engineering at RMIT University, said the outage appeared to be affecting the network layer in Microsoft’s systems, and was potentially similar to the configuration issue which brought down Optus’s network in 2023.

He said when some of the servers have configuration issues, it means customers are forced to share a smaller pool of servers to access the cloud services.

“If you lose part of that pool, then all of the users are trying to access the remaining servers, and that overloads them. And then they go down.

“And so you end up with a cascading problem. Then it takes time to find the underlying problem: in this case, a misconfiguration in the network layer.”

#66. Search for British woman missing from Tui cruise in Atlantic called off

The woman disappeared while on the Marella Voyager (file photo)

The search for a British woman who is believed to have gone overboard from a cruise ship while it was crossing the Atlantic has been suspended, the US Coast Guard (USCG) has said.

The 72-year-old was last seen aboard Tui's Marella Voyager on Monday while the vessel was en route to the Caribbean. Staff realised she was missing on Tuesday, when they noticed her "bed untouched".

USCG said the ship was approximately 500 nautical miles from the area where she was believed to entered the water when it received a missing report.

It added that a coordinated international search and rescue effort had been called off on Wednesday due to factors including "the low likelihood of survival".

USCG said the woman was last recorded accessing a cabin door at about midday on Monday. She is believed to have gone overboard in a remote area of the North Atlantic, roughly 1,400 nautical miles from Bermuda.

In a previous statement, Tui said that after staff realised she was missing, the alarm was raised and a search carried out.

The Daily Mail earlier reported that holidaymakers on board the ship had been told to abandon dinners, drinks and entertainment as the crew carried out an emergency headcount on Tuesday evening.

Passengers stood in lines of four while staff scanned boarding cards to verify the identity of every person on the ship, according to the newspaper.

In a statement, USCG confirmed it had received a missing report at about 19:30 EST (00:30 GMT) on Tuesday.

The coast guard said staff also reported that the Marella did not have enough fuel to return to the search area. It is understood that USCG asked the ship to continue its crossing rather than reverse course and search for the woman.

It said that volunteers from the coast guard then worked with search and rescue partners including the Maritime Rescue Coordination Center Delgada and the UK's Joint Rescue Coordination Centre, and issued urgent maritime broadcasts requesting assistance from ships in the area.

It added that multiple commercial vessels were contacted as part of the operation to "consider search opportunities, but none were in the vicinity".

Search efforts were suspended at about 15:50 EST (20:50 GMT) on Wednesday, "after considering the extensive search area, the elapsed time between the presumed incident and notification, the lack of available search assets in the remote location, negative results from maritime broadcasts, the sea state, and the low likelihood of survival", the coast guard said.

The Marella Voyager, carrying around 2,000 passengers and 800 crew, left Malaga on 4 November and is due to arrive in Antigua on Friday before calling at Dominica and finishing its voyage in Barbados on Sunday.

A spokeswoman for Marella Cruises earlier said it was supporting the authorities with their enquiries. It added it was "deeply saddened" by the disappearance, adding: "Our thoughts are with the guest's loved ones at this very difficult time.

A UK Foreign Office spokesperson said in an earlier statement: "We are supporting the family of a British national reported missing on a cruise ship in the Atlantic Ocean and we are in touch with the relevant authorities."

In a separate case, a cruise ship pianist was found treading water on Sunday around 20 miles off the Californian coast after falling overboard.

Two fishermen discovered the Royal Caribbean employee near Catalina Island and officials believe he may have been in the Pacific Ocean for nine hours, according to CBS News, the BBC's US news partner.

#64. Eight killed in Colombian air strike targeting Gulf Clan gang

Colombia's president said one of 10 "priority targets" was among those killed

A senior leader of Colombia's largest criminal gang, the Gulf Clan, is among eight people killed in an air strike carried out by the country's armed forces, according to President Abelardo de la Espriella.

De la Espriella said he had personally authorised the bombing targeting the Gulf Clan in the province of Antioquia, in north-west Colombia.

He added that Haminton Mosquera, whom he described as a senior member of the crime gang and one of 10 "priority targets" for Colombia's security forces, had been "brought down".

De la Espriella was elected in June after campaigning on a promise to crack down on Colombia's powerful criminal gangs, including by launching air strikes.

In a post on X, external, he said that Mosquera was suspected of having ordered attacks against the security forces, including the murder of a police officer in the municipality of TarazÃ¡ last year.

The Gulf Clan has been engaging in criminal activities for decades, mainly trafficking cocaine from Colombia to destinations in the US and Europe but also illegal mining and people smuggling.

The US added it to its list of Foreign Terrorist Organisations (FTOs) last year.

In his post, President de la Espriella said that "the offensive won't stop" and vowed to regain control of areas currently controlled by criminal organisations such as the Gulf Clan.

While his offensive against criminal gangs has been welcomed by many living in areas where extortion and gang violence are rampant, de la Espriella has faced criticism for the way he has made some of his announcements on security.

Less than a month after taking office, he published a video showing him walking among 23 body bags containing the remains of 23 people killed in an air strike on a suspected guerrilla camp.

On Wednesday, he shared a photo, external in which he can be seen writing his campaign slogan, "Welcome to the miracle homeland" on a bomb.

He has also defended his decision to wear an army uniform at a recent event in CÃºcuta at which he confronted a detainee suspected of being a Farc rebel.

"What did you make of the bomb I sent you, signed by me?," the president can be heard asking the man.

He later told journalists that he wore the uniform because "I am a soldier of the homeland. That's why I dress like a soldier, because I came to serve Colombia. Because I honour the heroes, police officers and military."

Additional reporting by BBC Monitoring's Luis Fajardo.

#62. Minister’s office said contractor should handle CFMEU or lose work in Victoria, Big Build inquiry hears

Royal commission also hears from construction company employee who says workers were ‘fearful’ of union

Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast

A major contractor on a troubled Victorian rail project shut down by union officials was warned that the minister’s office felt it would not “have any more work” in the state if it did not resolve the industrial dispute, a royal commission has heard.

The royal commission into the integrity of major public and civil infrastructure construction projects in Victoria heard from a Victorian Infrastructure Delivery Authority (Vida) official for the first time on Friday.

Vida was strongly criticised by counsel assisting the commission, Chris Young KC, on Thursday for its failure to disclose relevant evidence in a timely manner to the investigation.

The commission’s November public hearings, which are set to run until 20 November, are focusing on a 2022 level crossing removal project at Gap Road in Sunbury.

The commission previously heard that Construction, Forestry and Maritime Employees Union (CFMEU) officials shut down the site because of purported safety concerns, only for them to allow it to continue after two of their own health and safety representatives were appointed, and the project changed its traffic and labour hire companies to CFMEU-aligned firms.

Peter Wilkinson, a former Vida official who is now the executive general manager of the Suburban Rail Loop authority and a civil engineer, said that at the height of an industrial dispute at Gap Road he spoke about the need for work to resume on the project with Evan Tattersall, his manager at Vida.

He said Tattersall told him on either 6 or 7 June 2022 that he had discussed the issue with the minister’s office, and Wilkinson assumed that meant the office of the then-minister for transport infrastructure, Jacinta Allan.

Tattersall told him, Wilkinson said, “that if they can’t handle the industrial [relations] in Victoria, they can’t have any more work”.

Tattersall was referring to the contractors on the site, John Holland and CPB Constructions, Wilkinson said.

Wilkinson said he was aware at the time that the contractors were tendering for the airport rail, another major state government funded infrastructure project, but that he did not know if the minister’s office also knew this.

Wilkinson said Tattersall did not tell him what he should do with the information from the minister’s office, but he passed it on to Joe Aygur, the general manager of the Rail Network Alliance involved in the project’s delivery.

Aygur told the commission about the warning on Tuesday.

When asked by Young why no record of his conversation with Tattersall existed, Wilkinson said “probably one reason is that it was moving very quickly, so it was conversation that we had to keep it moving, rather than there being a need to formally record it.”

Wilkinson was also asked about a meeting scheduled in Allan’s parliament house office for 5pm on 7 June 2022 – the day work recommenced at Gap Road.

He was listed as an optional attendee on an online invite for the meeting, but said he could not recall if he attended.

The commission also heard on Friday from Stephen Villella, who worked for CPD Constructions and was the delivery director at the site, about the meeting at the union headquarters that ultimately ended the dispute.

He said it had been “extremely galling” for construction company officials to have to “bend the knee” by meeting with the CFMEU to resolve the matter.

Villella said none of the safety concerns raised by the union were serious enough to force work to stop at the site.

Workers on the project were fearful of the CFMEU and would stay in the lunchroom for long periods once they started to attend.

“They didn’t want to come [to work] at all,” Villella said.

“They were fearful of what would happen to them organisationally and individually if they did.”

Villella said he disagreed with evidence given early in the week by a former CFMEU organiser Paul Tzimas about those on site choosing not to work, saying they were heavily pressured by union officials.

Villella said that in the meeting with the CFMEU, a union organiser Joe Myles suggested collaborating would assist in winning future tenders such as the Melbourne airport rail project.

He said Myles also suggested he had direct lines to Allan’s office.

Villella said he was not surprised by Myles’ claim about having communication to the ministers office, as he had seen what had happened to the industry over the past 10 years.

Villella said Myles left abruptly in the first half of the about 30 minute meeting, which he said felt like a snub.

But he rejected a suggestion by counsel for Myles that it was not the union official, but rather Tzimas, who raised the list of union demands for the Gap Road project.

After construction managers agreed to the demands, work on the site resumed as normal.

“Amazingly, all our safety issues ceased,” Villella said.

Chris Carr SC, senior counsel assisting the commission, said that after the rebuke issued by Young on Thursday to Vida, he was “happy to report there has been substantial production of highly relevant material overnight”.

He said that production had led to several lines of inquiry, which meant the commission was unlikely to hear from witnesses again until Tuesday.

#61. U.S. Efforts to Tighten Blockade of Iran Rely on Reluctant Neighbors

Washington wants to intensify its economic pressure campaign on Iran by closing its land borders. Neighboring states, fearing economic fallout, are pushing back.

By Erika SolomonEric Schmitt and Tyler Pager

The first step in the United States’ campaign to economically strangle Iran was a naval blockade of its ports, cutting off Iranian trade by sea. Next came a raft of new sanctions, grounding much of the country’s air transport network.

Now, the United States is going after Iran’s last economic artery: its land routes. Only there, Washington has hit upon a problem.

For weeks, U.S. officials have tried — and failed — to diplomatically pressure Iran’s neighbors into shuttering their border crossings, according to three Iraqi officials and one U.S. official with knowledge of the talks, who requested anonymity to speak candidly.

Those neighboring states, primarily Turkey, Iraq and Pakistan, are increasingly wary and disgruntled by U.S. policies they say could hurt them as much as Tehran.

So, on Saturday, the U.S. military joined the effort, announcing Task Force Iron Gate, which it says will work on “preventing the movement of advanced weapons systems, dual-use technologies, sanctioned materials, and illegal revenue streams across Iran’s land borders and the Caspian Sea.”

It is unclear how the task force will operate in practice, and it is unusual for such an economic and security campaign to be run by U.S. Central Command, known as Centcom, which oversees military operations in the Middle East.
