#35. In China’s Spy Operations, Romance Can Be a Long Game

Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman U.S. officials say is a Chinese spy has drawn attention to the role of intimate relationships in espionage.

At the height of the Cold War, a top official in China’s intelligence agency said that his country would never use seduction to entrap a person.

Such tactics might be for the C.I.A. or the K.G.B., the official named Kong Yuan, of the Central Investigation Department, told a group of agents in training in April 1960, but China was guided by principles. He looked at the few female comrades in the audience. “Rest assured, we will not be asking you to go run beauty traps.”

Despite that pledge, captured in a 2010 memoir by a former Chinese diplomat, China’s intelligence services have for decades used romantic entanglements to compromise or extract information from prominent people.

American officials say that China’s Ministry of State Security, which runs intelligence operations today, recruited a Chinese businesswoman to collect intelligence on Jensen Huang. Mr. Huang, who resigned on Thursday as the chief executive of Nvidia, has been one of the most powerful figures in the artificial intelligence boom.

It was not clear what role the woman, Alice Huang, or Huang Le, might have had with the Chinese spy agency. Ms. Huang was a top executive of a Singapore company that operates data centers in Southeast Asia using Nvidia chips and that rents their computing power to Chinese firms.

Mr. Huang has acknowledged that he had an affair with Ms. Huang. President Trump has said that Mr. Huang told him that he was not aware that the woman was a spy. China has rejected the espionage allegations against Ms. Huang, calling them smears against the country. U.S. officials have not provided evidence for their claims. Ms. Huang has not responded to requests for comment.

#33. Falkland Islanders face ‘devastating consequences’ if Argentina blocks oil project, says UK

Countries clash over Sea Lion project as Hamburg tribunal risks deepening sovereignty dispute

The people of the Falkland Islands face “devastating consequences” if Argentina is successful in blocking development of its offshore oilfield, the attorney general for England and Wales has told an international tribunal.

Ellie Reeves, representing the British government at a hearing of the international tribunal for the law of the sea (Itlos), said billions of pounds vital to the future of the British overseas territory would be lost.

Reeves accused Argentina of surreptitiously seeking to pursue its longstanding sovereignty claims over the south Atlantic islands through an application to the tribunal, which is sitting over two days in Hamburg.

Argentina is seeking a provisional ruling suspending the drilling for oil before further arbitration over the future of the project, which it claims poses an environmental risk.

Reeves told the tribunal: “In reality, what Argentina opposes is hydrocarbon activities for the economic benefit of the Falkland Islanders. What it wants is to take control of those resources for itself.”

Argentina claims Britain’s approval of the Sea Lion project is in breach of a 1976 UN resolution calling on both parties to desist from unilateral action on the islands until the sovereignty dispute is settled.

Buenos Aires wants to force a commitment from the UK that it will “stop the drilling”, in order to “preserve the present situation” before a settlement over the oilfield.

After raising Argentina’s “armed invasion” of the islands in 1982, which she told the tribunal led to “loss of life and military occupation”, Reeves said the Falkland Islanders had a right to exploit their own natural resources, adding that a tribunal that ruled over the law of the seas had no place in settling a sovereignty dispute.

Reeves said that suspension of the drilling, which is due to start in March next year, would kill off the Sea Lion project.

“The provisional measures sought by Argentina would prevent the people of the Falkland Islands from developing their natural resources as they are entitled to do so, pursuant to their fundamental right to self-determination,” she said.

“The measures sought would have devastating consequences for the Falklands people. They risk losing billions of pounds in money, which is essential for the islands’ economic development.”

Sir Michael Wood KC, who also spoke on behalf of the UK, said the tribunal could lose member states should it overreach by issuing “provisional” measures that should be used only in exceptional circumstances.

Asked whether the UK would abide by the tribunal’s ruling, Reeves told the Guardian that she respected the institution but that she would not “get ahead” of the decisions.

Speaking earlier on Tuesday, Mario Oyarzábal, chief legal adviser to Argentina’s ministry of foreign affairs, had told the tribunal that the 3,600 inhabitants of the Falkland Islands, had no right to the resources around the south Atlantic archipelago.

Oyarzábal said: “[Argentina] respects the interests of the inhabitants of the islands and their way of life as enshrined in the Argentine constitution … What these British citizens do not have is a right to decide to which state the islands belong …

“These resources belong to the Argentine people. Some 46 million of them including the 1,247 inhabitants born on the islands whose interests will be duly taken into account.”

He said Argentina was ready to negotiate “all aspects of the question” of the Falkland Islands, which are known as the Malvinas in Argentina, “including the present aggravation of the dispute caused by the UK”.

He accused the UK of refusing to enter talks over the islands while pushing forward on the exploitation of its natural resources in a way that would entrench the status quo.

Oyarzábal claimed that the oil money from the drilling would “finance the very military base and presence that sustains the UK’s de facto control of the territory”.

Argentina’s case is that provisional measures to suspend the drilling are urgent. The Sea Lion project in the North Falkland basin, led by the Israeli firm Navitas and its British partner, Rockhopper, will start drilling in March and oil will be extracted from 2028 and continue for 35 years. It has the potential to double the islands’ GDP per capita to a cumulative uplift of £8bn over the lifetime of the project. The UK government asked why an application to Itlos had only been made in recent months amid increasingly bellicose rhetoric.

Argentina further claims that it has rights as the “coastal state” to the Falklands. The UK’s lawyers said this claim highlighted that Argentina’s application was “at heart” about sovereignty.

The Sea Lion project has been condemned as unlawful by Argentina’s president, Javier Milei. The tribunal was told by Argentinian lawyers that the permissions given by the UK government for drilling was the “unilateral conduct” of a “colonial power”.

Oyarzábal added: “In the event of a blowout or spill, it is Argentina that would bear its consequences and be called upon to respond, not a state that is 12,700km away.”

UK lawyers responded that the risk of such an incident was low and that the companies involved had mitigated against it.

Argentina invaded the Falklands in 1982 at the cost of the lives of 649 Argentinian military personnel, 255 British military personnel and three Falkland Islanders. The hearing is the first time that substantive matters relating to the islands have been litigated at an international tribunal. In a show of solidarity, Princess Anne arrived in Port Stanley on Monday. The hearing continues on Wednesday.

#32. Hegseth awards medal to war memorial guard who reprimanded him and group

Military cemetery decorum: The tradition behind Hegseth's Veterans Day reprimand

US Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth has awarded a medal to the guard who reprimanded his group at Arlington National Cemetery.

A video of a Veterans Day observance event on Wednesday showed Hegseth, his wife and senior advisers laughing and talking while posing for photos in front of the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier before the guard told them to be quiet.

In a statement, the Pentagon later announced that Hegseth had given Specialist Thomas Iglesias a Joint Service Commendation Medal "for the execution of his duties at the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier".

Photos shared on the defence secretary's X account showed him presenting the medal to Iglesias.

He was accompanied by others whom the Pentagon identified as members of the 3rd US Infantry Regiment, known as the Old Guard, which is responsible for holding vigil over the Tomb of the Unknown Soldier.

"We love soldiers. We love standards. Well done, Specialist. Carry on Patriots," the accompanying text on Hegseth's post reads.

In the video, the army sentinel on duty later identified as Iglesias can be seen stepping towards Hegseth's group and shouting: "All visitors will maintain an atmosphere of silence and respect at all times."

Hegseth (right) awarding the medal to Specialist Iglesias

The Arlington National Cemetery etiquette requests that visitors speak quietly and do not engage in disrespectful or disorderly conduct.

The tomb, which is one of the country's most sacred war memorials, marks the final resting place of an unknown soldier who was killed in World War One.

Chief Pentagon spokesman Sean Parnell said in a statement: "While commemorating this solemn day, a soldier correctly and respectfully reminded everyone to maintain decorum.

"The Secretary and the entire Department have always honoured - and will always honour - the sacrifices our military men and women have made to keep America safe."

A service member from the 3rd Infantry Regiment stands watch over the tomb 24 hours a day and has done so since 1948.

It is not unusual for them to admonish visitors to the site, according to the BBC's US partner CBS News. They reportedly do so a few times a day.

Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth and First Lady Melania Trump bow their heads in prayer during a Veterans Day ceremony at Arlington National Cemetery

Earlier, President Donald Trump, Vice-President JD Vance and other top officials attended the annual wreath-laying at the cemetery.

During his speech at the ceremony, Trump offered his gratitude to uniformed service members who have fought for the country. He also touted what he sees as his administration's success in the Iran war.

Trump also pointed out Hegseth during the ceremony, saying he's done a "fantastic job" as defence secretary.

On Tuesday, an effort to impeach the defence secretary under accusations that he waged war on Iran without congressional authorisation failed on Capitol Hill.

Hegseth has called the impeachment efforts a "farce" and a "stunt".

#30. Killing of journalist underscores deteriorating conditions in Ecuador

Tomás Beltrán was shot dead amid gang warfare that has turned Latin American country into one of its most violent

Tomás Beltrán was manoeuvring his car into his garage on Sunday night when the Ecuadorian journalist was shot dead.

Beltrán ran a Facebook page with news about Calceta, a small city in the province of Manabí, whose Pacific coastline has made it a key flashpoint in the gang warfare over drug trafficking routes that has transformed one of Latin America’s safest countries into one of its most violent.

Police and the public prosecutor’s office have not said much about the case, and little was known about the killers except that they were armed and then fled the scene. It was also unclear whether the crime was linked to Beltrán’s work, though he had previously reported allegations of corruption involving local authorities.

Regardless, many press freedom groups already cite his murder as the latest example of how conditions for journalists have deteriorated dramatically in Ecuador. Six journalists were killed in the country last year alone.

Artur Romeu, Reporters Without Borders’ Latin America director, said in a statement that months ago Beltrán had told the organisation “about his fears and the threats he was receiving”.

“We took part in efforts to get him to safety,” he said, adding that Beltrán had been placed in a protection programme run by the public prosecutor’s office, although it was not known whether that protection was still in place when he was killed.

“Reporters Without Borders calls on the public prosecutor’s office to investigate as a priority whether there is a link to his journalistic work, identify any possible masterminds and protect his family and colleagues,” said Romeu.

The Ecuadorian civil society organisation Fundación Periodistas Sin Cadenas (FPSC) reported that at least 19 journalists have had to go into exile since gang violence exploded in 2023. Although Ecuador does not produce cocaine like neighbouring Colombia and Peru, it has become a major trafficking hub, particularly through the Pacific.

Journalists have received death threats with photographs of their homes and details of their routines and families.

“Practising journalism in Ecuador is now an extremely high-risk job,” said María Fernanda Almeida, a project coordinator at FPSC, adding that all of last year’s killings of journalists “remain completely unpunished”.

“And one of the many terrible effects of this violence is self-censorship: at least in the coastal provinces, colleagues no longer want to report on violence,” she added.

Almeida said another problem for journalists has been the stance of the government of the far-right president Daniel Noboa, who has responded to the surge in crime with a controversial “iron-fist” approach to the gangs, repeatedly declaring states of emergency and expanding the role of the armed forces in measures that have been widely criticised by human rights groups.

While covering military operations, reporters have had their phones confiscated, a search warrant was executed at a reporter’s home and a journalist was threatened by soldiers simply for filming them stopping university students.

There have also been several reports that Noboa, one of US president Donald Trump’s key allies in Latin America, has pressured media organisations to dismiss journalists critical of him, including the columnist Roberto Aguilar, who said he was fired from the newspaper Diario Expreso after the government reportedly threatened private advertisers with audits if they continued advertising with the outlet.

Noboa’s administration did not respond to requests for comment.

“I don’t think there is complete freedom of expression in Ecuador today,” said Almeida. “And that is very worrying because the more violence and silencing there is, the more journalism is needed.”

#28. Australian footballers will not be charged after hotel incident, police say

Isaac Heeney is among the players police say will not be charged over an incident at a hotel in August

Four top Australian Football League (AFL) players who were investigated in relation to an alleged sexual assault at a hotel in Melbourne earlier this year will not be charged, police have said.

After a "thorough investigation and based on legal advice" police would not proceed with criminal charges due to "insufficient evidence", police said in a statement.

Four Sydney Swans players - Isaac Heeney, James Jordon, Chad Warner and Nick Blakey, who now plays for North Melbourne - had been suspended for the rest of the season over the incident in August.

The men previously denied wrongdoing but apologised for breaching club standards by inviting women back to the hotel after a game and drinking.

Australian media reported that three women, understood to be adult entertainers, had alleged inappropriate touching during the incident at the Pullman Hotel and are looking into civil action.

"Due to the ongoing civil matter, Victoria Police is unable to make further comment," police said in a statement on Friday.

A fifth player, Riley Bice, who was also suspended by the club, had already been cleared by police.

A lawyer for one of the women said she was "devastated" by the police decision.

Jackson Pannam, of Slater and Gordon law firm, added that the move "does not amount to a finding that the incident our client reported did not occur, nor does it diminish the seriousness of what she says happened or its profound impact on her".

John Karantzis from Carbone Lawyers, acting for the other two women, told the ABC his clients were "very disappointed and distressed".

They would "vigorously pursue" a civil action, he said.

"My clients' civil claim is based on a different burden of proof, it is based on the balance of probabilities," he said.

Heeney welcomed the police announcement, saying the past three months had been "incredibly difficult for my family, [my fiancee] Steffie and those close to me".

"To my family and friends, your love and loyalty has meant more than words can do justice," he continued.

The Swans said in a statement that it had been "clear from the outset" that its decision to suspend the players was "separate from the police investigation, and it stands by that decision".

"The Club has commenced a comprehensive examination of its AFL program and is committed to acting on the findings and implementing meaningful change to build an environment that players, staff, members, supporters and corporate partners can be proud of," it said.

Swans CEO Matthew Pavlich told reporters it was "not a day for anyone to really celebrate" and "we're still looking very hard at the programme and understanding where we need to get better".

He said the club would be doing everything to "restore the standards that this club has held for a long period of time".

In a separate statement the AFL acknowledged the police decision and said it reserved the right to investigate should any further relevant information come to light.

#27. They Left Iran, but Now the Regime Is Seizing Their Assets to Silence Them

The Iranian authorities are pursuing diaspora dissidents and journalists, extending the government’s repressive apparatus far beyond its borders.

Yeganeh Torbati spoke to 12 Iranians living abroad whose property in Iran, or that of their family members, was targeted for confiscation.

The retired couple in Iran first realized that something was wrong when their bank cards stopped working in early May. After inquiring with the authorities, they discovered that their assets had been frozen by order of the country’s intelligence ministry.

The couple learned, eventually, that they were being punished for the actions of their daughter Noushin Najafiragheb, who lived in Europe — specifically, for her social media posts opposing the Iranian government, according to another daughter, Mehrnoush Najafiragheb.

Across Iran, the government has used asset seizures as punishment for actions taken abroad. Homes and businesses have been seized, life savings raided, family heirlooms taken. When those targeted had little inside Iran themselves, the authorities went after their family members back home, interviews show.

As the Najafiragheb family pressed their case with the authorities, Mehrnoush said, they encountered dozens of other people facing the specter of losing their life’s savings as punishment for the actions of their relatives abroad.

“We realized we weren’t alone,” Ms. Najafiragheb recalled her mother telling her. She said her sister Noushin had declined to be interviewed to avoid further government pressure on her family.

#25. Argentinian lawyer mocks UK’s sovereignty claim in Falklands oil case

Lawyer says Britain wrong to believe in historic doctrine that crown has sovereignty over parts of oceans

Britain should not be permitted to act as a 17th-century colonial power treating the waters around the Falkland Islands as a Dominium maris Britannici, Argentina has told an international tribunal.

Marcelo Kohen, representing Argentina at a hearing in Hamburg, mocked Britain for apparently believing in the historic doctrine that the British crown has sovereignty over expansive parts of the oceans.

Argentina has applied to the international tribunal for the law of the sea (Itlos) for a ruling that would force Britain to suspend the drilling for oil in the North Basin of the Falkland Islands ahead of further arbitration.

The Sea Lion oilfield is about 130 miles north of the Falkland Islands, a British overseas territory over which Argentina has a longstanding sovereignty claim.

Argentina’s president, Javier Milei, reportedly shared among his cabinet on Tuesday a photograph of the Argentinian legal team in Hamburg, describing it as “tremendous”, adding that “we are working on the Falklands issue like no one else in history”.

Addressing the tribunal on the second day of hearings, Kohen said that the dispute over the archipelago of 700 islands in the south Atlantic – known in Argentina as the Malvinas – did not mean that the area was a “legal black hole” and that the UK could do as it wished with its natural resources.

“The fact that the parties dispute sovereignty does not turn the areas surrounding the Malvinas into some kind of Dominium maris Britannici,” he said. “We are not in the 17th century … We are in an era where we should put an end to the last vestiges of colonialism.”

Ellie Reeves, the attorney general for England and Wales, told the tribunal’s panel of judges on Tuesday that Argentina was seeking to further its sovereignty ambitions by applying for suspension of the drilling.

She claimed that such a move would kill the Sea Lion project, with “disastrous consequences” for the Falkland Islanders and the companies involved. The UK claimed that the tribunal, which adjudicates over maritime matters, does not have jurisdiction to wade into matters of sovereignty.

Kohen, switching from French to English, said the UK had failed to mention in its submissions the “elephant in the room” or the “whale in the bathtub”. The UN regarded the Falkland Islands as a non-self-governing territory, he said, and had called in 1976 for both sides to refrain from taking decisions that would unilaterally change the situation before the sovereignty dispute was settled.

The exploitation of hydrocarbons would put the islands among the “highest ranks of wealth just below the principality of Monaco” while depriving Argentina of its rights to those resources, Kohen told the tribunal.

Argentina’s legal team claimed that suspension of the drilling, which is due to start in March next year and which could double the GDP of the islands, was urgent given the irreparable damage to Argentina’s rights.

In his closing remarks to the panel of 22 judges, Mario Oyarzábal, a legal adviser to Argentina’s ministry of foreign affairs, said they rejected the UK’s attempt to characterise the proceedings as an attempt to litigate the sovereignty dispute.

He said: “The question before this tribunal is a much narrower one: whether, while the underlying dispute remains unresolved, the UK may unilaterally authorise or carry out new drilling for the exploitation of hydrocarbons in the disputed maritime area.”

Sam Wordsworth KC, responding for the UK, said there was nothing in the convention on the law of the seas, over which Itlos has jurisdiction, which obliged a state not to “aggravate” a sovereignty dispute and that the UN resolution cited by Kohen as the “elephant in the room” was not legally binding.

The UK further claims there is no need for urgent suspension of drilling as oil extraction would not take place until 2028, before which an arbitration panel would have come to a conclusion on Argentina’s wider claim that its rights have been breached.

During his recent speech at the UN general assembly, Milei described the reclaiming of the Falklands as a “national cause”. Argentina invaded the islands in 1982 on the orders of the dictator Gen Leopoldo Galtieri. The 74-day conflict ended with the surrender of the Argentinian forces and cost the lives of 649 of its military personnel, 255 British military personnel and three Falkland Islanders.

Itlos is expected to make its ruling on Argentina’s case on 28 November.

#23. Suspected bomb hoax threats force fresh evacuations of Italian schools

Bomb squads have searched many schools but no devices have been found

Schools across Italy have received bomb threats for the second day in a row, sparking fresh panic and evacuations.

Some 3,778 schools, from Milan in the north to Sicily in the south, reported similar threats to one that created chaos on Wednesday - even though no explosive devices have been found.

The government has advised schools that "in the absence of concrete evidence beyond the simple receipt of a mass email indicating a danger, buildings must not not be evacuated". Unlike a day earlier, only 77 schools were evacuated on Thursday, authorities say.

Prime Minister Giorgia Meloni says her government will come up with "sector-specific" advice to help those facing this kind of threat.

Schools in Scotland and Northern Ireland received threats on Wednesday, too. It is unclear whether the incidents are connected.

On Thursday morning, a bomb alert at a Calais rail station delayed international and national train services in northern France.

The latest incidents follow a series of bomb hoaxes last week against airports and airlines in a number of European countries.

Although a number of threats were claimed by an extremist misogynistic group, it later denied any link to the false warnings.

Wednesday's email threat - sent to 6,300 out of a total of 7,476 Italian state schools - was said to be from a certain "Mikhail Andropov, leader of Vilayat di Misoginistan".

Some officials say it originated in Taiwan.

A similar message sent to Italian schools on Thursday, again signed by Mikhail Andropov, is said to have been sent from the account of a teacher in the Philippines whose account is believed to have been hacked.

No risk to public from hoax school bomb threats, says government minister

Police treating threatening email sent to more than 300 schools as 'hoax'

Many parents, though, are concerned and have been demanding clarity and action from the government.

La Repubblica newspaper reports that protests have been held against a decision by the headmaster of a secondary school in Rome who did not take any action, saying she had realised the threatening message to contain typos and therefore be "fake".

According to the newspaper, protesters want new national guidelines to be issued.

#22. Ex-member of radical Marxist group scales balcony, escapes house arrest in Argentina

Leonardo Bertulazzi, 74, awaiting extradition to Italy, reportedly used harness to flee custody

A 74-year-old former member of Italy’s radical Marxist group the Red Brigades has escaped from house arrest in Argentina, the country’s security ministry confirmed on Wednesday, after he reportedly hid his ankle monitor inside a tube of pork and lowered himself from a fifth-floor balcony.

Authorities placed Leonardo Bertulazzi under house arrest in Buenos Aires in 2024, prior to his extradition to Italy to serve a 27-year prison sentence. He had received the sentence in 1987 for joining a subversive association and belonging to an armed group.

After years on the run, Bertulazzi was first arrested by Argentine authorities in 2002. He was freed a few months later and secured refugee status in 2004, but was arrested again in 2024 after it was revoked.

On Wednesday, Argentina’s security ministry offered a $13,000 reward for information leading to Bertulazzi’s recapture after he slipped out of detention.

The fugitive held a senior position in the Red Brigades, a radical Marxist organization responsible for attacks from 1969 to 1980, dubbed Italy’s “Years of Lead”. The group most notoriously kidnapped and assassinated former prime minister Aldo Moro in 1978.

Security footage showed Bertulazzi dropping from a building into a public road as cars drove past.

A detective tracking the former member of the Red Brigades reportedly told La Nación that Bertulazzi rappelled down from the fifth floor using a harness.

“First, he removed his electronic ankle monitor and placed it in a tube filled with pork, along with a blood pressure monitor to maintain the temperature. He then climbed on to a roof and, using a rope, lowered himself five meters until he jumped onto a vacant lot behind the building where he lived, and then scaled a metal gate to reach the street,” the detective said, according to the publication.

The footage, which indicated the time was 6.01am on 10 November, showed a possibly shaken Bertulazzi sitting down to rest for a few moments.

Police noticed his ankle monitor had stopped transmitting movement data and headed to the house, where they found the device discarded and Bertulazzi nowhere to be found.

Argentina approved his extradition to Italy in July 2025 after the country’s supreme court rejected a final appeal.

François Picard is pleased to welcome Victor Casallo, Director of the Department of Philosophical and Theological Studies at Antonio Ruiz de Montoya University. Can a Pope shaped by Peru's history of political violence, social inequality and religious activism help a deeply polarized nation heal and rediscover the possibility of dialogue? "We tend to see the other person not as someone who holds a different opinion, but as an enemy." Casallo argues that Pope Leo, the first American-born pontiff's identity cannot be understood through his Chicago origins alone. His decades of pastoral experience in Peru, amid political instability, terrorism, state violence and profound social inequalities, have shaped a religious outlook rooted in cultural openness, solidarity with the marginalized and an intimate understanding of human suffering.

#20. Two skulls likely belonging to enslaved people returned to Brazil after Harvard used them in eugenics studies

Remains believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians repatriated after being flown back by diplomats

Two skulls, including that of a man believed to have taken part in Brazil’s greatest slave revolt but whose remains were taken to the United States and used in racist eugenics studies at Harvard University, were repatriated on Wednesday.

The remains had been outside Brazil since the 19th century. One was taken about 190 years ago from Salvador, the capital of the state of Bahia; the other, about 160 years ago, from Rio de Janeiro – and were welcomed back in moving, subdued ceremonies in both cities.

The skulls are believed to have belonged to Africans or Afro-Brazilians in the country that received more enslaved Africans than any other during the transatlantic slave trade.

Each was brought back in a padded box inside a reinforced suitcase, carried on flights by Brazilian diplomats after a four-year campaign to recover the remains from Harvard’s Peabody Museum of Archaeology and Ethnology in the US.

Brazilian researchers from local universities will now try to learn more about who the two individuals were.

The skulls will not be displayed in museums or have their images released, and the Brazilian government has pledged that they will be buried with funeral rites as soon as the identification process is complete.

“It’s very important to restore the dignity of these people, which was taken from them in life and in death,” said Carolina Dias dos Reis, a government official who travelled to Boston to sign the agreement with Harvard.

The existence of the skulls was revealed in 2022, and Brazilian researchers sought their repatriation from Harvard, but said they faced a drawn-out process that only moved forward after the Brazilian government became involved in late 2024.

In recent months, concerns grew that Donald Trump’s sometimes hostile behaviour towards his Brazilian counterpart, Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, could derail the repatriation. But everything worked out.

“We need to recover our past as a country, however difficult and violent it was, so that we can understand what we are living through in the present: the consequences and scars of structural racism,” added Reis.

Slavery was abolished in 1888, but nearly a century and a half later, Afro-Brazilians make up the majority of the population while occupying the bottom of every socioeconomic indicator, dominating the statistics on hunger, poverty, gender-based violence and victims of homicides committed by criminals or the police.

During the repatriation ceremony in Rio de Janeiro, Ivanir dos Santos, a history professor and a cleric of the Afro-Brazilian religion Candomblé, poured a glass of water on to the ground as researchers received the suitcase.

“It was a ritual act because you cannot deal with the remains of our ancestors without paying reverence to them. This is not a research object … for us, that person is still alive in memory and as our ancestor,” he said.

It is not precisely known who the skulls belonged to. The one taken to Salvador is believed to have belonged to a man who took part in the most significant urban slave revolt in Brazilian history, in 1835, when about 600 African Muslims attempted to seize control of what was then the country’s second most important city. They were ultimately defeated by police, who killed 70 rebels.

The man died at a hospital, and a US diplomat, Gideon T Snow, is believed to have sent the skull to Boston.

The Bahia Islamic Cultural Centre, one of the most active campaigners for its repatriation, has named the man Arakunrin, meaning brother in Yoruba.

Even less is known about the other person: initially, only that they were “an individual … exhumed from the ‘streets of Rio de Janeiro’” in the 1860s. Brazilian government officials, however, said on Wednesday that Harvard researchers had told them that some features – including that the skull was smaller than that of an adult man but larger than that of a child – suggested it may have belonged to a woman.

Bruno Veras, a historian who was part of the group that closely followed the repatriation, cross-referenced diaries, letters and maps of the city and believes the skull may have been taken from a cemetery for newly arrived Africans who either did not survive the long Atlantic crossing or died soon after disembarking.

The person believed to have taken it to the US was Walter Hunnewell, a student from a wealthy Boston family. He was part of an expedition led by the Harvard professor Louis Agassiz, who was in Brazil trying to document physical types to support his racist theories.

“To steal a skull and take it to another country was violent even in the context of racist research,” said Veras.

Researchers in forensic anthropology will now try to confirm the sex of the individuals and other information through measurements of the skulls – the one in Salvador is complete, while the one in Rio is missing its jaw – and microscopic analyses. If necessary, they will seek authorisation to carry out genetic or dating tests.

“This history of slavery cannot be undone,” said Irina Feisthauer Silveira, a diplomat who carried one of the skulls. “But acknowledging the mistakes of the past with transparency is a first step towards building a more equal, just and less violent future.”

#18. Russian warship hit by Ukrainian drone in Gulf of Finland

The Naro-Fominsk is believed to have headed back to port in St Petersburg after the attack

Finnish officials have confirmed that a Russian warship was attacked in the Gulf of Finland on Wednesday, although little is known about the strike.

Ukraine's SBU security service said it targeted the ship Naro-Fominsk as part of a broader drone operation against Russia's Ust-Luga oil export depot on the Baltic coast.

The attack, thought to be the first time Ukrainian drones have attacked a Russian ship in an area of the Baltic Sea open to civilian maritime traffic, has raised questions about whether Ukraine could expand its strikes.

Helsinki has been one of Kyiv's closest European allies, and Finnish officials have described the incident as a direct result of Russia's continued full-scale war.

Finland's military has given few details of the incident, but it appears to have taken place in a narrow stretch some distance from the Finnish coast.

The country's Foreign Minister Elina Valtonen was adamant that the situation in the Baltic Sea remained stable and safe and that there was no threat of any kind to marine traffic.

Defence officials are understood to have stressed to Kyiv that the Gulf is a relatively narrow waterway used by oil tankers and civilian vessels, and is also home to subsea data cables.

Ukraine's SBU described the Russian ship as a Buyan-M type small corvette that carried Kalibr and Oniks cruise missiles. There were also unconfirmed reports of Russian combat helicopters being destroyed.

Martin Herem, the defence minister of Estonia, which borders the Gulf, told public broadcaster ERR that the ship was hit at about 05:30 (03:30 GMT) on Wednesday but did not say how it was attacked: "We know that smoke came from the ship afterwards. The ship itself went back towards St Petersburg."

The extent of casualties or damage sustained by the Russian navy ship remains unclear, and Moscow rarely gives such details.

"The Baltic Sea fleet has been untouched up to this point but there seemingly was a window of opportunity for Ukraine and they understandably tried to use it," said military analyst Emil Kastehelmi, co-founder of the Black Bird monitoring group.

Russian and Ukrainian vessels have been repeatedly targeted in the Black Sea since Russia's full-scale invasion began in February 2022, but even though the latest strike on a ship in the Baltic is unprecedented, Kastehelmi told the BBC he did not consider it a serious escalation.

Ukrainian drones are regularly launched into Russia from northern Ukraine. Several storage tanks at the Ust-Luga terminal are thought to have been hit overnight on Wednesday and fires at the port were visible over the border in Estonia.

Herem said Ukrainian drones attacked the Ust-Luga region from midnight until about 06:00, mostly flying close to the Estonian border but still in Russian airspace.

He added that one drone had entered Estonian airspace briefly from Latvia and then headed towards Russia.

Nato aircraft based in the Baltic states were in the air at the time, but Herem said it had not been considered safe for them to destroy the drones.

Ukrainian foreign ministry spokesman Heorhiy Tykhyy said late on Wednesday that Moscow's allegations that Kyiv had used the airspace of the Baltic states to attack Russia were "baseless", although he acknowledged "incidents might occur when Russia diverts drones from its airspace using electronic warfare".

#16. Kenyan doomsday sect leader jailed for 200 years for murder of 190 children

Paul Mackenzie convicted over deaths of children and one adult after followers were ordered to starve themselves and their families

The leader of a Kenyan doomsday sect has been sentenced to 200 years in prison for the murders of 190 children and one adult in one of the world’s worst cult disasters in recent history.

The sentence, comprising 100 years for murder by starvation and 100 years by direct homicide, came two weeks after the court in Kenya’s coastal city of Mombasa found the cult leader guilty of the crimes.

Prosecutors said Paul Mackenzie and his Good News International church ran ​a Christian doomsday sect whose members were ordered to starve themselves and their children to death to go to heaven before the world ended.

His accomplices threatened and beat victims to ensure they followed his orders. The emaciated bodies were found scattered in shallow graves and dumped in latrines. Some had been buried alive.

Mackenzie was arrested in 2023 after police received a tipoff, and weeks later authorities began uncovering bodies in shallow graves in the Shakahola forest near the coast in south-eastern Kenya.

In total, more than 400 bodies have been recovered. Many were severely decomposed and only 26 could be identified.

Mackenzie denied murder charges, saying he did not force the victims to starve, but the court rejected that argument.

The Shakahola tragedy has gripped Kenya since the discovery of mass graves in the forest more than three years ago.

It raised questions about how Mackenzie was able to continue operating despite being on the radar of the authorities since 2017, when police searched his premises.

In 2019, authorities shut down Mackenzie’s church and he relocated ‌to the Shakahola forest, police said.

The case has also led to calls for tougher oversight of churches and other religious organisations in a country where religious faith plays a prominent role in public life.

Mackenzie, a former taxi driver, forbade cult members from sending their children to school and from going to hospital when they were ill, describing such institutions as satanic.

Cult members lived in makeshift homes of polythene sheeting and thatch that Mackenzie divided into areas with biblical names such as Jerusalem and Judea.

The plight of his followers began ​to emerge in March 2023, when a man told ​police his brother and wife had starved their children ​to death in the forest on Mackenzie’s orders.

Mackenzie was arrested but released on bail, after which relatives said he brought forward the date ​on which he told his followers the ‌world would end. Police rearrested him and ​started combing through the forest more systematically and began exhuming mass graves.

Last month’s judgment covered 191 deaths while investigations into more than 200 other deaths are continuing.

Justice Diana Kavedza-Mochache said in a hearing lasting more than eight hours that in some cases children were isolated and denied food while their parents watched.

Mackenzie faces other charges over the forest deaths, including those of terrorism, which he has denied.

Prosecutors said that while he was in detention he also directed the deaths of 52 people in Kwa Binzaro village, about 18 miles from Shakahola. Mackenzie has pleaded not guilty in that case.

#15. She Is Rewriting Mexico’s Ballads, One Woman’s Story at a Time

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Grammy-nominated songwriter Vivir Quintana is known throughout Latin America for giving voice to women who fought back against domestic violence.

Against the backdrop of a centuries-old stone church in the Mexican city of Oaxaca, the singer Vivir Quintana raised her deep, husky voice above the blare of trumpets, trombones and the booming pulse of a tuba.

Behind her stood a brass band of young Indigenous girls in embroidered white blouses and brightly colored skirts. Hat in hand and fist raised, Ms. Quintana sang a corrido, a traditional Mexican folk ballad usually used to recount tales of revolutionaries, outlaws, folk heroes, and more recently, drug traffickers.

Corridos are typically sung by men, about men. Ms. Quintana sang a corrido she wrote based on the true story of a woman who fought back when her abusive partner tried to gouge out her eyes with a knife.

“I used to think that what united us women was our pain. Now I have realized it is love and resistance,” Ms. Quintana told the crowd in October during a women’s conference in Oaxaca called Decididas, drawing a roar of applause. “We must keep defending ourselves!”

On Thursday, Ms. Quintana will take the stage at the Latin Grammy Awards in Las Vegas alongside the Colombian pop superstar Juanes, with whom she is nominated for song of the year for “Humano.” It is her second Latin Grammy nomination.

#14. North Korean hackers used fake video calls in $400m crypto hack, says FBI

FBI redacted examples of the 900 victim videos secretly recorded by North Korean hackers

State-sponsored hackers from North Korea have taken more than $400 million in cryptocurrency by using fake video calls to steal people's identities, US authorities say.

More than 900 victims have fallen prey to the scheme, which the FBI refers to as FaceStealer, in the last 18 months, according to the bureau.

"The North Korean regime relies on proceeds from large-scale cryptocurrency theft to fund its illicit weapons program, stealing billions of dollars in recent years," says FBI cyber director Brett Leatherman.

This comes a week after the world's largest crypto exchange Binance revealed that hackers had used fake job interviews to steal a record $6.3bn (Â£4.7bn) from the company.

US law enforcement agencies attributed the Binance hack, thought to be the largest crypto hack to date, to North Korea-linked actors. Pyongyang has denied this claim.

Authorities say FaceStealer, the latest scheme, "aggressively targets" those who work in the cryptocurrency and web3 sectors, particularly those who are involved in the development and maintenance of open-source software.

According to the FBI and US Justice Department, FaceStealer hackers use fake or hijacked social media accounts to invite potential victims into video calls hosted over "imposter versions" of Zoom, Microsoft Teams, and Google Meet.

Next, the hackers play pre-recorded videos of previous victims the target is acquainted with to simulate a legitimate call â except that the audio would appear to malfunction.

A message then pops up on the target's screen, asking them to "fix" the audio by clicking on a link. This "fix" would eventually download malware onto the target's device, which allows hackers to access their contact lists and apps, widening their pool of potential victims.

Hackers would secretly record these calls and use the recordings as material to scam their new victims, according to the US authorities.

The FBI has described FaceStealer as "a highly effective social engineering scheme capable of deceiving even the most experienced technical and security conscious computer user".

"It exploits our trust in ubiquitous video conference platforms and valued personal relationships," Patrick Grandy, assistant director in charge of the bureau's Los Angeles field office, said in a statement on Thursday.

"If you can't imagine a scenario where you might fall prey to it, you are not thinking creatively enough."

#10. How African philosophies inspired a new approach to staging film festivals in Manchester

See My World, an event highlighting the pan-African thought via film, poetry and dance, builds on momentum sparked by local activists and creatives

African philosophies including exploring how lessons from the past can shape the present and near future have inspired a film festival coming to Manchester.

See My World, a day dedicated to independent creators from across the African diaspora in the north of England, will take place at the Contact theatre on 21 November.

The festival is part of See My World’s year-round programme showcasing pan-African arts, culture and events.

It reflects the determination among creatives and activists in Manchester to build on the legacy of the 1945 Pan-African Congress in Manchester – which connected luminaries of postwar independence movements with a shared vision of liberation – after last year’s 80th anniversary commemorations.

See My World’s CEO and artistic director, Tunde Adekoya, said this month’s film festival was taking inspiration from across the spectrum of pan-African thought, including the works of Kenyan writers John Mbiti, an Anglican theologian who died in 2019, and Ngũgĩ wa Thiong’o, the author of the groundbreaking 1986 book Decolonising the Mind who died last year.

Both writers rejected the colonial conceit that African thought was inferior, stressed the importance of community and saw reclaiming African ways of knowing as essential to a people’s self-understanding.

While Mbiti sought common ground between African religions and Christianity, Ngũgĩ was a Marxist. He argued colonialism was motivated by cultural and economic conquest, and that European powers used language as their sharpest instrument. Ngũgĩ famously abandoned English to write in Gikuyu, a Bantu language mainly spoken in Kenya and criticised the role missionary Christianity played in colonial rule.

Adekoya said: “It’s a day dedicated to pan-African and global majority films from the north of England with exclusive premieres of documentaries we’ve been working on, which weave together expressions of liberation through art forms like poetry and dance.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

“The whole idea is that it’s like a playground in search of strategies of liberation, using the festival as a way to explore different philosophies.

“John Mbiti explores the philosophy and spiritual understandings of time through the concept of Sasa na Zamani – in which the past informs everything that happens moving forward. That reflects how we constantly go back to pan-African histories to inform how we progress.”

Film-makers have been invited to submit films lasting up to 30 minutes from across genres, with no screening fees charged.

As well as submissions, the festival will feature documentaries produced by See My World. They include Homage, a “cinematic love letter to dance” that traces a culture through present-day portraits illustrating dance artists and archival material from Manchester, telling a story of joy, loss, migration, rhythm and resistance. Meanwhile, Poetic Justice explores how pan-African thought and Manchester’s poetry scene speak across decades.

Adekoya added: “The idea is that we would go against the norm of how film festivals are, because when we think about decolonisation, we think about how structures have been created to alienate and exclude communities.

“So we thought our film festival would look more at how we make film accessible and how stories that deserve to be seen get seen. Throughout the day we will have different activities, from exhibitions to explorations of our performances, discussions, Q&As, insights into directors’ processes, and workshops.

“We’ve been looking for literature and concepts that inform us and give us an understanding of who we truly are. And as works from thinkers like John Mbiti have come into our sphere, they have really emboldened us to think differently about our place in the world and what we do.”

See My World film festival runs from 2pm to 11pm on Saturday 21 November at Contact theatre, Oxford Road, Manchester.

#8. A.I. Weapons Need Data for Their Training. Ukraine Has a Lot to Offer.

The Ukrainian government has opened up a trove of five million battlefield videos to defense companies at home and abroad.

The forest, seen from a drone, appeared as a vast, monotonous landscape of black tree trunks, shadows and patches of snow.

To the human eye, it appeared deserted. But to an artificial intelligence system looking for people, it did not. The system put a red box around a tiny human form, probably a soldier lurking in the trees.

Behind this feat lay vast volumes of data. Just as ChatGPT and other A.I. chatbots must be exposed to how humans write, military A.I. systems must be exposed to how humans fight. In Ukraine, this happens through a huge trove of battlefield videos, audio and text data collected over nearly five years.

The war database is being used by about 30 Ukrainian defense companies to develop A.I. weapons. Ukraine is now opening this repository, which it calls Avenger Labs, to firms in allied countries, hoping to deepen security partnerships.

Ukraine is providing the data at no charge, but companies must give the Ukrainian military licenses to any software they develop. Under the first agreement to be reached, 12 British defense companies will gain access. Ukraine is in talks with other countries.

While there is no shortage of Western companies developing autonomous weapons, there is a shortage of the data needed to train them. Ukraine can offer more of it than any other potential partner.

#7. Zelensky welcomes US bringing Europe into Russia-Ukraine peace talks

US envoys Jared Kushner (left) and Steve Witkoff (right) met Volodymyr Zelensky in Dublin on Thursday evening

Ukraine's president has welcomed a pledge from US envoys, Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff, to include delegates from the European Union in the next round of peace talks with Russia.

European leaders have expressed deepening security concerns this week, and Volodymyr Zelensky said bringing them into the talks would ensure the EU has a "voice" in negotiations.

Kushner and Witkoff met Zelensky in Dublin on Thursday evening as part of a busy day of shuttle diplomacy - they met Vladimir Putin in Moscow earlier, who said that Russia's position on the war - and its demand for territory - has not changed.

The meetings follow talks in Abu Dhabi last week that failed to reach a breakthrough in ending the war.

But Thursday did see Russia and Ukraine agree to another prisoner exchange - the largest swap yet since Russia launched its full-scale invasion in 2022.

Kushner said it would return "thousands of people to their families", but further details like how many prisoners would be exchanged or a timeline, were not given.

US envoys meet Zelensky in Dublin after talks with Putin in Moscow

Europe's security 'fundamentally reshaped' by war in Ukraine

Zelensky has for several years been calling for European delegates to be included in the peace talks, and says it makes sense because of Kyiv's longer-term ambition to join the European Union.

On Thursday, Kushner said that the US has been "working very, very closely over the past several months with Europeans and I think that that's been very important."

He added that he had stressed to Putin how important it is to have European delegates "at the table".

"He's willing to give it a try," Kushner added.

Ukraine and Europe have long been concerned about the current US government's commitment to European security and its softening towards the Kremlin.

Moscow has been dismissive of European involvement, although Europeans have long argued that they are central to any post-war dynamics on their continent.

Earlier on Thursday, Vice President of the European Commission Kaja Kallas, the EU's top diplomat, told BBC's Newshour the presence of a European delegate showed the Kremlin "now realised that Europe is a big player".

Kallas also expressed doubts that there was Russian momentum towards a peace deal.

Taoiseach (Irish Prime Minister) MicheÃ¡l Martin told a host of Euro leaders gathered in the Irish capital on Thursday that he believed the security of the region has been "fundamentally reshaped" by Russia's war.

"Many member states [of the EU] feel an existential threat because of what has happened in Ukraine and the potential, if Ukraine was to fall, for further aggression on the European continent," he told reporters.

Approximately one-fifth of Ukraine remains under control of Russian forces, and cross-border incursions are increasingly common.

Tensions between the US and Ukraine were on display on Thursday before Witkoff and Kushner's meeting with Zelensky.

Earlier, the Ukrainian leader said that the military was facing more "systematic" restrictions to Starlink's military-grade satellite service, Starshield, meaning Kyiv was struggling to intercept Russian jet-powered drones.

The technology owned by Elon Musk's SpaceX company has played a vital role in government and military connectivity in Ukraine, and increasingly assists in facilitating communication on Ukraine's front line.

#6. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

CNN’s Paula Reid contributed reporting to this post.

Senate Homeland Security Chairman Gary Peters said he has not seen any indication that protests in Washington, DC, over former President Donald Trump’s indictment will become dangerous.

“I think you’re always going to be vigilant, so folks will be vigilant. Security and local law enforcement are certainly monitoring it,” he told reporters Monday.

Peters, who is also a member of Senate Democrat leadership, said it is unclear what effect the indictment will have on the Senate. “We don’t know what’s gonna transpire tomorrow, let alone what’s going to happen in the few weeks and months. I think we’d have to take a look at what the indictment is all about.”

Former President Donald Trump has departed Florida and is en route to New York to face criminal charges.

His personalized Boeing 757 took off from the Palm Beach International Airport this afternoon at about 12:59 p.m. ET ahead of his Tuesday appearance in a Manhattan courtroom to be arraigned.

Trump, the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history, is expected to land at New York’s LaGuardia airport around 3 p.m. ET, according to a source familiar with his plans. He will stay at Trump Tower tonight, the source told CNN.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, according to two sources familiar with the case. A New York grand jury handed down the sealed indictment late last week. The charges have yet to be made public.

The former president is not expected to stay in New York City for very long. Immediately after his Tuesday court appearance, Trump plans to return to Florida, the source said, where he has scheduled an event to speak publicly.

CNN’s Alta D. Spells and Jack Forrest contributed reporting to this post.

New York City Mayor Eric Adams warned GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene to “be on your best behavior” after the Republican firebrand said she will travel to the city to protest former President Donald Trump’s indictment.

Adams made the statement at a press conference as New York security officials detailed plans ahead of Trump’s arrival in Manhattan for his arraignment scheduled on Tuesday.

“While there may be some rabble rousers thinking about coming to our city tomorrow, our message is clear and simple: Control yourselves. New York City is our home, not a playground for your misplaced anger. We have the safest large city in America because we respect the rule of law in New York City. And although we have no specific threats, people like Marjorie Taylor Greene, who is known to spread misinformation and hate speech — she stated she is coming to town. While you’re in town, be on your best behavior,” Adams said.

“As always, we will not allow violence or vandalism of any kind. And if one is caught participating in any act of violence, they will be arrested and held accountable. No matter who you are,” he added.

#5. Collision of two trains in Egypt kills at least seven

President orders prompt investigation after one train derails in collision in al-Ayyat about 50km from Cairo

Seven people were killed and at least 125 injured when two trains collided on Sunday south of the Egyptian capital, Cairo, officials said.

The crash occurred in al-Ayyat district in Giza province, about 50km (30 miles) south of Cairo, when a train travelling to Fayoum province crashed into the rear of a train heading to the southern province of Aswan, according to the transport ministry.

An inspection indicated that the Cairo-Aswan train had been stationary on the same track, officials said.

The health ministry gave the casualty toll, adding more than 70 of the injured were discharged after receiving treatment.

Train derailments and crashes are common in Egypt, where the railway system has a history of mismanagement and poor maintenance of equipment. In recent years the government has announced modernisation initiatives to improve its railways.

Ayman Gamal, a resident of Kafr Girza in al-Ayyat where the crash occurred, said people rushed to the scene, pulled out survivors and called ambulances.

Gamal said he saw about 80 of the injured being taken by ambulances to hospital. Some passengers were trapped inside one of the carriages of the stationary train and died, he said.

Egypt’s president, Abdel Fatah al-Sisi, said in a social media post that he had ordered a prompt investigation.

Outside al-Wasta central hospital in Beni Suef province, where some of the injured were taken for treatment, survivors said passengers were in shock and struggling to breathe because of all the dust that was kicked up. Nearby villagers eventually got them out, they said.

Farha Gamal, a survivor who was inside the Fayyoum train, said she saw some of the passengers who appeared dead and others were struggling to find their way out of the car. “There were other cars that were completely mangled,” she said in an interview.

Emergency teams and cranes were dispatched to the crash site to clear the wreckage and restore train services to their normal schedule as soon as possible, the Egyptian railway authority said.

Last September, a train driver and his assistant were injured after two freight trains collided in the same district.

Egypt’s deadliest train crash was in 2002 when more than 300 people were killed after a fire broke out on an overnight train journeying from Cairo to southern Egypt.

#4. Ex-Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang summoned to testify before Congress about affair with alleged Chinese spy

How could the Nvidia CEOâs affair threaten US national security?

Former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has been summoned to testify next month before a Senate committee about his alleged affair with a woman suspected of being a Chinese spy.

Huang abruptly quit on Thursday as head of one of the world's most valuable companies after revelations of alleged extramarital affairs, in a stunning fall from grace that sent shockwaves through Silicon Valley.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who is under scrutiny over exports of sensitive Nvidia AI chips to China, has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December.

Nvidia has lost $550bn (Â£415bn) in stock market value in just five days as the scandal dominated headlines.

Senator Tim Scott sent letters on Thursday to Huang and Lutnick requesting their testimony in front of the Senate Banking Committee.

"Recent reports raise serious concerns about our national security and the protection of sensitive US technology," the South Carolina Republican posted on X.

"The American people deserve answers," he added.

Scott asked Huang to appear voluntarily on 3 December, but also warned the panel could issue a subpoena to compel his testimony.

Three House of Representatives committees - on intelligence and foreign affairs and a panel on China - have also sent a letter to Huang summoning him for a closed-door interview on 1 December.

How AI visionary Jensen Huang was undone by his own bad decisions

The rise and fall of Jensen Huang - how the tech superstar became mired in scandal

Lutnick is under scrutiny because unnamed officials in President Donald Trump's administration have been briefing US media that the commerce secretary stalled efforts to tighten export restrictions on advanced AI chips to China.

A commerce department spokesperson told US media that Lutnick had "proactively" agreed to appear before the Senate Banking Committee.

Huang, who has been Nvidia's public face since 1993, admitted on Sunday to having been "unfaithful" to his wife after reports he had an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, Alice Huang.

On Wednesday, he was reported to have had a second relationship, this time with an Nvidia executive.

Alice Huang (no relation) worked as an executive at the Singaporean firm Megaspeed. She has been suspected of being an intelligence asset for China, according to the New York Times. China has rejected that allegation as a smear.

Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick has also been summoned to appear before the Senate Banking Committee in December

US Attorney General Todd Blanche said on Thursday the Department of Justice had not yet drawn any conclusions about whether Alice Huang was engaged in espionage for Beijing.

Nvidia officials have repeatedly declined to comment since Wednesday. A personal representative of Huang also declined to comment on Thursday.

According to US media, Huang's alleged second affair was with a long-time Nvidia executive, Kimberly Powell, who reported directly to him within the company.

Sources have told the BBC that Nvidia employees had noticed the pair were unusually close.

Who is Nvidia's interim CEO Colette Kress - and who could take over permanently?

Huang had been widely expected to weather the storm after the first affair became public, even as Nvidia announced it had launched an investigation into whether its CEO had been "the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

One current Nvidia staff member told the BBC that the initial reports of his infidelity were more or less brushed off by rank-and-file workers inside the company.

It wasn't until Huang's alleged affair with the Nvidia executive became public that workers started to feel "this is crazy", the person said.

Another Nvidia employee said they had been told not to speak with the press or otherwise comment on the situation in public.

Many staff have earned millions of dollars in compensation through their ability to cash out their Nvidia shares as the stock soared.

Nvidia has said it has "no reason to believe that either national security or Nvidia secrets were compromised", but pledged that its independent inquiry would look at all available information.

The company's meteoric rise had been led by Jensen Huang, who became a major booster of the AI industry, which relies on Nvidia for the powerful computer chips it manufactures.

Initially those chips were designed for computer gaming.

But as AI technology evolved in the last four years into chatbots and large language models, companies building AI tools suddenly required huge numbers of the Nvidia computer chips that could process large amounts of data.

Nvidia is now hunting a permanent replacement for Huang. Chief Financial Officer Colette Kress was named on Thursday as interim CEO.

Several tech insiders that the BBC spoke with this week noted that Huang's downfall has been a major topic of conversation in San Francisco Bay area circles, an area filled with tech employees, industry executives and billionaires.

Huang has been married to Lori Huang for 40 years. Both of their children are also executives at Nvidia.

Nvidia's stock fall this week is the most significant five-day drop the company has seen since going public 27 years ago.

Its market value rose by more than 470% in the past five years, peaking just a few weeks ago. It is now down by 31% against a backdrop of wider uncertainty in the tech sector.
